<a id='question-126'></a>

### Question 126: Central Nervous System Pathology
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Transient ischemic attack (TIA) is caused by temporary focal neurological deficits resulting from transient cerebral ischemia, typically due to small vessel occlusion or embolism, without acute infarction. (短暂性脑缺血发作 (TIA) 是由小血管闭塞或栓塞引起的短暂性脑缺血导致的暂时性局灶性神经功能缺损，没有急性梗死。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of hypertension, hyperlipidemia, and type 2 diabetes mellitus presents to the emergency department after experiencing sudden onset of right-sided weakness and slurred speech that lasted for 35 minutes before completely resolving. He denies headache, fever, or loss of consciousness. His vital signs are stable. Neurological examination is now normal. A non-contrast head CT scan performed immediately after symptom onset was negative for hemorrhage or acute infarction. Carotid Doppler ultrasound reveals a 70% stenosis of the left internal carotid artery. An MRI of the brain performed 24 hours later shows no evidence of diffusion restriction or acute ischemic changes. Given the patient's presentation and investigations, what is the most likely underlying pathophysiological mechanism responsible for his symptoms?

(A) Vasculitis leading to focal vessel inflammation and transient occlusion.
(B) Cardioembolic event causing temporary blockage of a small cerebral artery.
(C) Lacunar infarct formation due to chronic hypertension-related small vessel disease.
(D) Migraine with aura causing transient focal neurological deficits.
(E) Seizure activity leading to Todd's paralysis.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia, diabetes). This increases the likelihood of cerebrovascular disease.
- **Symptoms:** Sudden onset of right-sided weakness and slurred speech (focal neurological deficits).
- **Duration:** Symptoms lasted 35 minutes before complete resolution. This duration (< 24 hours, often < 1 hour) is characteristic of a Transient Ischemic Attack (TIA).
- **Negative Investigations:** Non-contrast head CT was negative for hemorrhage or acute infarction immediately after symptoms. MRI 24 hours later showed no diffusion restriction (no acute infarct). This confirms the transient nature of the ischemia.
- **Carotid Doppler:** 70% stenosis of the left internal carotid artery. This identifies a potential source of emboli that could cause transient cerebral ischemia.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the most likely underlying pathophysiological mechanism.

The combination of transient focal neurological deficits, resolution within hours, negative acute imaging for infarction, and the presence of a significant carotid stenosis strongly points towards a TIA caused by a temporary embolic event originating from the stenotic carotid artery, occluding a small cerebral artery.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **TIA Definition:** A TIA is defined as a transient episode of neurological dysfunction caused by focal brain, spinal cord, or retinal ischemia, without acute infarction. Symptoms typically resolve within 24 hours, often much sooner (e.g., < 1 hour).
- **Pathophysiology:** The most common cause of TIA is thromboembolism, often originating from atherosclerotic plaques in the carotid arteries or the heart (cardioembolism). The embolus travels to the brain and temporarily occludes a small artery or arteriole, leading to ischemia in the corresponding brain region. When the embolus dislodges or breaks up, blood flow is restored, and symptoms resolve.
- **Mechanism in this Case:** The patient has significant carotid stenosis (70%), a known source of emboli. The sudden onset and resolution of focal neurological deficits (right-sided weakness, slurred speech) within 35 minutes, coupled with negative imaging for acute infarction, are classic features of a TIA caused by a cardioembolic or carotid embolic event. The embolus likely originated from the stenotic carotid plaque, traveled to a branch of the middle cerebral artery (MCA) supplying the motor cortex and Broca's area (explaining the right-sided weakness and slurred speech), caused temporary occlusion, and then resolved.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Vasculitis:** Vasculitis (e.g., giant cell arteritis, polyarteritis nodosa) can cause stroke or TIA-like symptoms, but it typically involves inflammation of blood vessel walls, potentially leading to stenosis, occlusion, or aneurysm formation. While it can cause transient symptoms, it's less common than thromboembolism, and the presentation often includes systemic symptoms (fever, weight loss, myalgias) or specific features related to the type of vasculitis. The vignette doesn't suggest systemic inflammation.
- (C): **Lacunar Infarct:** Lacunar infarcts are small (<1.5 cm) infarcts in the deep structures of the brain (basal ganglia, thalamus, pons, internal capsule) caused by occlusion of small penetrating arteries (lenticulostriate arteries) due to lipohyalinosis or microatheroma, typically associated with chronic hypertension. While the patient has hypertension, a lacunar infarct is a *permanent* ischemic lesion, resulting in persistent neurological deficits. The vignette explicitly states the symptoms resolved completely, and imaging showed no infarct.
- (D): **Migraine with Aura:** Migraine with aura can cause transient focal neurological symptoms (visual, sensory, motor, speech disturbances) that typically develop gradually over minutes and last less than 60 minutes. While it can mimic TIA, the symptoms are usually different in character (e.g., scintillating scotoma, paresthesias), often preceded by headache, and are not typically associated with significant vascular risk factors or carotid stenosis. The sudden onset and specific motor/speech deficits in this patient are more suggestive of ischemia.
- (E): **Todd's Paralysis:** Todd's paralysis is a transient focal neurological deficit (usually weakness) that occurs *after* a seizure, typically lasting from minutes to hours. It is thought to be due to postictal cortical hyperexcitability or metabolic changes. The vignette does not mention any seizure activity, making this diagnosis unlikely.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature             | TIA (Correct Answer B)                               | Vasculitis (A)                                  | Lacunar Infarct (C)                           | Migraine with Aura (D)                          | Todd's Paralysis (E)                            |
|----------------------|------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|
| **Cause**           | Embolism (carotid/cardiac) or Thrombosis in situ      | Inflammation of vessel walls                    | Occlusion of small penetrating arteries         | Cortical spreading depression                   | Postictal cortical hyperexcitability/metabolic |
| **Symptoms**        | Sudden onset, focal deficits                          | Variable, often systemic + focal                 | Sudden onset, focal deficits (deep structures) | Gradual onset, variable (visual, sensory, motor) | Occurs *after* seizure                          |
| **Duration**        | Transient (< 24 hrs, often < 1 hr)                   | Variable                                        | Permanent deficit                               | Transient (< 60 min)                           | Transient (minutes to hours)                    |
| **Imaging (Acute)** | Usually normal (no infarct)                          | May show inflammation, stenosis, aneurysm        | Shows small infarct                             | Normal                                          | Normal                                          |
| **Risk Factors**    | Atherosclerosis, cardiac disease, hypertension, DM    | Variable, depends on type                       | Hypertension, DM, atherosclerosis              | Family history, triggers                        | Epilepsy                                        |
| **Vignette Fit**    | Excellent (risk factors, sudden onset, transient, neg imaging, carotid stenosis) | Poor (no systemic signs)                       | Poor (symptoms resolved, no infarct)            | Poor (sudden onset, specific deficits, risk factors) | Poor (no seizure history)                       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Transient ischemic attacks (TIAs) are critical warning signs of impending stroke, most commonly caused by emboli from atherosclerotic carotid artery disease or the heart. Differentiating TIA from stroke mimics like migraine with aura, seizures (Todd's paralysis), and less common causes like vasculitis is crucial. The key features of TIA are transient focal neurological deficits resolving completely within 24 hours, typically without evidence of acute infarction on imaging. Key differentials include lacunar infarct (permanent deficit), migraine with aura (different symptom evolution), and Todd's paralysis (post-seizure).

<a id='question-127'></a>

### Question 127: Central Nervous System Pathology: Focus 03-27 (atheroemboliand is characteristically reversible, w)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Atheroembolic disease affecting the central nervous system (CNS) can present with transient ischemic attacks (TIAs) or stroke-like symptoms, often characterized by neurological deficits that wax and wane or resolve completely, reflecting the intermittent nature of emboli release. The underlying pathology involves cholesterol crystal deposition in small arteries, leading to inflammation and occlusion. (动脉栓塞性疾病影响中枢神经系统 (CNS) 可表现为短暂性脑缺血发作 (TIA) 或类似中风的症状，通常表现为神经功能缺损时好时坏或完全消退，这反映了栓塞物间歇性释放的性质。潜在的病理机制是在小动脉中沉积胆固醇晶体，导致炎症和闭塞。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 72-year-old male with a history of hypertension, hyperlipidemia, and recent cardiac catheterization with stent placement presents to the emergency department with acute onset of left-sided weakness and slurred speech. These symptoms began approximately 3 hours ago and have fluctuated in severity, improving somewhat after 1 hour before worsening again. His vital signs are stable. Neurological examination reveals moderate left hemiparesis (strength 3/5) and dysarthria. Non-contrast head CT is unremarkable. CT angiography (CTA) of the head and neck shows no large vessel occlusion. A repeat neurological examination 6 hours after symptom onset reveals significant improvement, with left hemiparesis now mild (strength 4/5) and dysarthria resolving. Serum creatinine is elevated at 2.1 mg/dL (normal range 0.6-1.2 mg/dL). Given the fluctuating neurological symptoms, recent invasive procedure, and renal insufficiency, what is the most likely underlying pathological process responsible for his presentation?

(A) Cardioembolic stroke due to atrial fibrillation
(B) Lacunar infarct secondary to chronic hypertension
(C) Vasculitis affecting small cerebral arteries
(D) Atheroembolic disease affecting the CNS
(E) Migraine with aura complicated by hemiplegia

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 72-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia) and recent cardiac catheterization (invasive procedure).
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Acute onset, fluctuating neurological deficits (left hemiparesis, dysarthria) suggestive of transient ischemic events or a stroke with waxing/waning symptoms. The fluctuation is a key clue.
- **Timeline:** Symptoms began 3 hours ago, improved, then worsened, and significantly improved by 6 hours. This dynamic course is highly characteristic.
- **Imaging:** Non-contrast CT is negative (rules out large hemorrhage). CTA shows no large vessel occlusion (makes typical embolic stroke less likely, although small vessel emboli might not be seen).
- **Laboratory:** Elevated creatinine (2.1 mg/dL) suggests renal involvement, which can be associated with certain systemic processes.
- **Connecting Clues:** The combination of recent invasive procedure (cardiac catheterization), fluctuating neurological symptoms (waxing and waning), negative large vessel occlusion on CTA, and renal insufficiency strongly points towards atheroembolism. The procedure likely dislodged atherosclerotic plaque material, which then embolized to small arteries in the brain and potentially the kidneys.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Atheroembolism:** This occurs when fragments of atherosclerotic plaque (cholesterol crystals, cellular debris) break off from larger arteries (often the aorta or arteries recently manipulated during procedures like catheterization) and travel downstream.
- **Target Vessels:** These emboli typically lodge in small arteries and arterioles, leading to ischemia and infarction in the affected tissue. In the CNS, this often involves penetrating arteries supplying the basal ganglia, thalamus, pons, cerebellum, and cerebral cortex. Renal arteries are also commonly affected.
- **Clinical Manifestations:** The symptoms depend on the location of the emboli. CNS manifestations include TIAs, stroke-like symptoms, cognitive changes, visual disturbances, and seizures. Renal involvement can cause acute kidney injury or chronic kidney disease.
- **Characteristic Feature:** The "blue toe syndrome" (ischemia of the toes) is a classic sign, but CNS and renal involvement are also common. The fluctuating nature of neurological symptoms ("cholesterol shower") is due to the intermittent release and lodging of emboli.
- **Pathology:** Histologically, affected vessels show cholesterol clefts within the vessel wall and lumen, often surrounded by inflammatory cells (giant cells, macrophages).
- **Reversibility:** Unlike thrombotic or embolic strokes involving larger vessels, atheroembolic events affecting small vessels are often partially or completely reversible as the emboli break down or move distally, and collateral circulation develops. This aligns with the patient's improving symptoms.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Cardioembolic stroke due to atrial fibrillation:** While atrial fibrillation is a common cause of stroke, the symptoms are typically abrupt in onset and persistent, not fluctuating. Also, the recent cardiac catheterization and renal insufficiency are less directly related to atrial fibrillation as the primary cause, although AFib could coexist. CTA would likely show a large vessel occlusion if it were a cardioembolic stroke, or the symptoms would not typically resolve so dramatically within hours.
- (B): **Lacunar infarct secondary to chronic hypertension:** Lacunar infarcts are small, deep infarcts caused by occlusion of small penetrating arteries, often due to chronic hypertension (lipohyalinosis). While hypertension is present, the fluctuating nature of the symptoms is not typical for a lacunar infarct, which usually presents with a fixed neurological deficit. The recent procedure is also not directly causative.
- (C): **Vasculitis affecting small cerebral arteries:** Vasculitis (e.g., polyarteritis nodosa, Churg-Strauss) can affect small cerebral arteries, causing stroke-like symptoms. However, vasculitis typically presents with more systemic symptoms (fever, weight loss, rash, myalgias) and often involves multiple organ systems. While renal involvement can occur, the strong temporal association with the recent cardiac catheterization makes atheroembolism more likely. Also, vasculitis often causes more persistent deficits.
- (E): **Migraine with aura complicated by hemiplegia:** Hemiplegic migraine can cause transient hemiparesis, but it is usually associated with typical migraine headache features (photophobia, phonophobia, nausea) and often has a more predictable pattern. The fluctuating symptoms over hours, the patient's age and vascular risk factors, the recent procedure, and the renal insufficiency make migraine less likely than an embolic event.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Atheroembolism (D)                                  | Cardioembolic Stroke (A)                         | Lacunar Infarct (B)                           | Vasculitis (C)                                  | Hemiplegic Migraine (E)                      |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- |
| **Onset**               | Often after procedure, can be insidious or acute   | Abrupt                                           | Abrupt                                         | Variable, often subacute                        | Gradual or abrupt, often with headache        |
| **Symptom Course**      | Fluctuating, waxing/waning ("cholesterol shower") | Persistent, fixed deficit                         | Persistent, fixed deficit                      | Variable, often persistent                      | Transient, often resolves within hours/days     |
| **Vessel Size**         | Small arteries/arterioles                          | Large or medium arteries                          | Small penetrating arteries                      | Small/medium arteries                           | N/A (functional vascular change)               |
| **Associated Findings** | Recent procedure, renal insufficiency, "blue toes" | Atrial fibrillation, valvular disease, thrombus   | Chronic hypertension, diabetes                  | Systemic symptoms (fever, rash, etc.), organ involvement | Headache, aura, photophobia, phonophobia       |
| **Imaging**             | Often negative on CTA/MRA for large vessel occlusion | May show large vessel occlusion                  | Often normal on CT/MRI initially                | May show vessel wall thickening/stenosis        | Usually normal neuroimaging                    |
| **Pathology**           | Cholesterol clefts, giant cells, inflammation      | Thrombus, embolus                                | Lipohyalinosis, microatheroma                   | Inflammation, necrosis, granulomas              | N/A                                            |
| **Reversibility**       | Often partially/completely reversible               | Less likely to be fully reversible                | Usually irreversible                            | Variable, depends on treatment/severity          | Usually reversible                              |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Atheroembolic disease is a crucial differential diagnosis for patients presenting with fluctuating neurological deficits, especially after invasive vascular procedures. The characteristic waxing and waning symptoms, often accompanied by renal insufficiency or peripheral embolization ("blue toe syndrome"), should raise suspicion. Key differentials include cardioembolic stroke, lacunar infarct, vasculitis, and migraine, each with distinct clinical features and underlying pathology. Remember that atheroembolic events affecting small vessels are often reversible.

<a id='question-128'></a>

### Question 128: Central Nervous System Pathology: Focus 03-28 (less <24 hours.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathophysiology and characteristic histological findings of hypoxic-ischemic brain injury, specifically liquefactive necrosis and the timing of these changes. (理解缺氧缺血性脑损伤的病理生理学和特征性组织学发现，特别是坏液性坏死和这些变化的发生时间。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and atrial fibrillation (on warfarin) is found unresponsive at home by his daughter. Emergency medical services arrive 3 hours after the estimated time of collapse. His vital signs are stable, but he remains comatose (Glasgow Coma Scale score of 6). A non-contrast head CT scan shows no evidence of hemorrhage. He is immediately taken to the emergency department where a CT angiography reveals a complete occlusion of the left middle cerebral artery (MCA). Despite attempts at mechanical thrombectomy, reperfusion is unsuccessful. The patient remains neurologically devastated. An autopsy is performed 22 hours after the onset of symptoms. Microscopic examination of the affected MCA territory reveals neuronal cell death, edema, and liquefaction necrosis with loss of cellular detail and nuclear karyorrhexis. Astrocytes show reactive changes.

Which of the following pathological findings is MOST characteristic of the brain tissue in the affected MCA territory at the time of autopsy?

(A) Coagulative necrosis with preservation of cellular outlines and eosinophilic cytoplasm.
(B) Fibrinoid necrosis of vessel walls with transmural inflammation.
(C) Liquefactive necrosis with formation of a cystic cavity containing cellular debris and inflammatory cells.
(D) Petechial hemorrhages and hemosiderin deposition indicative of chronic microvascular ischemia.
(E) Demyelination with oligodendrocyte loss and microglial proliferation.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with risk factors (hypertension, atrial fibrillation on warfarin) for stroke.
*   **Clinical Presentation:** Sudden unresponsiveness, coma (GCS 6), found 3 hours after onset.
*   **Imaging:** Non-contrast CT negative for hemorrhage. CT angiography confirms left MCA occlusion.
*   **Intervention:** Unsuccessful mechanical thrombectomy.
*   **Outcome:** Neurologically devastated.
*   **Autopsy Timing:** 22 hours after symptom onset.
*   **Autopsy Findings:** Microscopic examination of affected MCA territory shows neuronal cell death, edema, liquefaction necrosis (loss of cellular detail, nuclear karyorrhexis), reactive astrocytes.
*   **Question:** Asks for the MOST characteristic pathological finding at autopsy (22 hours post-onset).

The key elements are the ischemic stroke (MCA occlusion), the timing (22 hours post-onset), and the microscopic findings described (neuronal death, edema, liquefaction necrosis). The question asks for the *most characteristic* finding. Liquefactive necrosis is the hallmark of ischemic injury in the brain. The timing (22 hours) is well within the period where liquefaction necrosis is established.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Ischemic Stroke Pathophysiology:** Interruption of blood flow leads to oxygen and glucose deprivation. This causes ATP depletion, failure of ion pumps (Na+/K+ ATPase), influx of Na+ and Ca2+, cellular edema, and ultimately neuronal death.
*   **Necrosis in the Brain:** Unlike most other organs where ischemia typically causes coagulative necrosis (protein denaturation preserves cell outlines), the brain undergoes liquefactive necrosis. This is due to the high lipid content and enzymatic activity (especially from released lysosomal enzymes and inflammatory cells) within the brain tissue, which leads to digestion of the dead cells and liquefaction.
*   **Timing of Changes:**
    *   Within minutes: Neuronal dysfunction, ion pump failure.
    *   Within hours (up to 12-24 hours): Irreversible neuronal injury, edema, early signs of necrosis (pyknosis, karyorrhexis, karyolysis).
    *   By 24-72 hours: Well-established liquefactive necrosis, formation of a cystic cavity filled with cellular debris, inflammatory cells (neutrophils, macrophages), and breakdown products.
    *   Later: Glial scar formation (astrocytes).
*   **Liquefactive Necrosis:** Characterized by enzymatic digestion of dead cells, resulting in a liquid, viscous mass. Histologically, this appears as loss of cellular architecture, nuclear changes (karyorrhexis, karyolysis), and eventual formation of a cystic space filled with necrotic debris and inflammatory cells.
*   **Relevance to Vignette:** The autopsy was performed 22 hours after onset, which is the typical timeframe for observing well-developed liquefactive necrosis in the brain following ischemia. The description in the vignette (neuronal death, edema, liquefaction necrosis, loss of cellular detail, nuclear karyorrhexis) directly points to this process.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Coagulative necrosis** is characteristic of ischemic injury in most solid organs (e.g., heart, kidney) but *not* the brain. It involves protein denaturation, preserving cell outlines for a time. This is incorrect for brain ischemia. Test-takers might confuse the general concept of necrosis with the specific type seen in the brain.
- (B): **Fibrinoid necrosis** is typically seen in immune reactions involving blood vessels (e.g., vasculitis, malignant hypertension) or in severe hypertension. It involves deposition of fibrin-like material in vessel walls. This is not the primary pathology of ischemic stroke itself, although vascular damage can occur. Test-takers might associate stroke with vascular pathology but confuse the type of necrosis.
- (D): **Petechial hemorrhages and hemosiderin deposition** indicate chronic microvascular ischemia or previous hemorrhagic events. While microhemorrhages can occur in acute ischemic stroke (especially in the peri-infarct zone or due to reperfusion injury), they are not the *primary* or *most characteristic* finding of the main infarct core at 22 hours, and hemosiderin deposition implies a more chronic process. Test-takers might confuse acute ischemic changes with chronic ischemic changes or hemorrhagic stroke.
- (E): **Demyelination** is characteristic of diseases affecting myelin (e.g., multiple sclerosis, leukodystrophies). While white matter tracts are affected in stroke, the primary pathology is neuronal infarction and liquefactive necrosis, not primary demyelination. Test-takers might confuse white matter involvement in stroke with primary demyelinating diseases.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Liquefactive Necrosis (Brain Ischemia) | Coagulative Necrosis (e.g., Myocardial Infarction) | Fibrinoid Necrosis (Vasculitis) | Petechial Hemorrhages/Hemosiderin (Chronic Ischemia) | Demyelination (MS) |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------ | :--------------------------------------------------- | :----------------- |
| **Tissue**              | Brain                                   | Heart, Kidney, Spleen, Liver                       | Blood Vessels                  | Brain (Chronic)                                      | CNS White Matter   |
| **Cause**               | Ischemia, Abscess                       | Ischemia                                           | Immune Reactions, Hypertension | Chronic Ischemia, Hemorrhage                         | Autoimmune        |
| **Mechanism**           | Enzymatic digestion                     | Protein denaturation                               | Fibrin deposition, Inflammation | Microvascular damage, RBC breakdown                   | Immune attack on myelin |
| **Histology**           | Loss of architecture, cystic cavity     | Preservation of outlines (initially), eosinophilia | Eosinophilic, amorphous debris | Small hemorrhages, iron pigment                      | Oligodendrocyte loss, microglial proliferation |
| **Timing (Brain)**      | Hours to days                           | Not applicable (wrong type)                        | Not applicable (wrong type)    | Days to weeks/months                                  | Variable           |
| **Vignette Relevance** | **MOST CHARACTERISTIC**                  | Incorrect type for brain                            | Incorrect type for brain        | Incorrect timing/primary finding                      | Incorrect primary pathology |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The brain uniquely undergoes liquefactive necrosis following ischemia due to its high lipid content and enzymatic activity. This contrasts with coagulative necrosis seen in most other organs. Remember the timing: liquefaction is well-established by 24 hours post-ischemic event. Key differentials include coagulative necrosis (other organs), fibrinoid necrosis (vasculitis), and demyelination (MS).

<a id='question-129'></a>

### Question 129: Central Nervous System Pathology: Ischemic Stroke
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathological changes occurring in the brain following an ischemic stroke, specifically the cellular and molecular events leading to neuronal death and the characteristic histological findings. (本题考察缺血性脑卒中后脑组织发生的病理变化，特别是导致神经元死亡的细胞和分子事件以及特征性组织学发现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 72-year-old male with a history of atrial fibrillation (on warfarin), hypertension, and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute onset of right-sided weakness and slurred speech, starting 3 hours prior to arrival. His NIH Stroke Scale (NIHSS) score is 18. Non-contrast head CT performed immediately shows no evidence of hemorrhage. The patient is treated with intravenous alteplase (tPA) within the therapeutic window. Twenty-four hours after the onset of symptoms, an MRI of the brain reveals a well-defined area of restricted diffusion in the left middle cerebral artery (MCA) territory on DWI sequence, consistent with an acute ischemic infarct. A subsequent brain biopsy performed 7 days post-stroke shows a wedge-shaped area of necrosis with loss of cellular detail, eosinophilic cytoplasm, and pyknotic nuclei in the affected neurons and glial cells. Microglial cells are proliferating at the periphery of the infarct.

Which of the following pathological findings is MOST characteristic of the neuronal injury observed in this patient 7 days after the acute ischemic stroke?

(A) Synaptic depression and decreased neuronal excitability due to impaired glutamate reuptake.
(B) Formation of a glial scar composed primarily of astrocytes and oligodendrocytes.
(C) Liquefactive necrosis with abundant neutrophils and macrophages.
(D) Neuronal death mediated by caspase activation following mitochondrial dysfunction and excitotoxicity.
(E) Demyelination with preserved axons and neuronal cell bodies.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 72-year-old male with risk factors for stroke (atrial fibrillation, hypertension, hyperlipidemia).
- **Presentation:** Acute onset right-sided weakness and slurred speech (left MCA territory symptoms).
- **Timeline:** Symptoms started 3 hours ago, treated with tPA.
- **Imaging:** Non-contrast CT negative for hemorrhage. MRI (DWI) shows acute ischemic infarct in the left MCA territory.
- **Biopsy:** Performed 7 days post-stroke. Shows wedge-shaped necrosis, loss of cellular detail, eosinophilic cytoplasm, pyknotic nuclei (hallmarks of coagulative necrosis in the brain), microglial proliferation.
- **Question:** Asks for the MOST characteristic pathological mechanism of neuronal injury 7 days after the stroke.

The key clues are the clinical presentation consistent with an ischemic stroke in the MCA territory, the confirmation by MRI, and the description of the brain biopsy findings 7 days later. The biopsy description (wedge-shaped necrosis, eosinophilic cytoplasm, pyknotic nuclei) is characteristic of coagulative necrosis in the brain following ischemia. The question asks for the underlying *mechanism* of neuronal death in this context.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Ischemic Stroke Pathophysiology:** Ischemia leads to a cascade of events. Initially, there's a failure of energy production (ATP depletion) due to lack of oxygen and glucose. This disrupts ion homeostasis (Na+/K+ pump failure, influx of Ca2+ and Na+, efflux of K+), leading to cellular swelling (cytotoxic edema).
- **Excitotoxicity:** Excessive release of glutamate occurs due to reversed glutamate transporters. This overstimulates postsynaptic glutamate receptors (NMDA, AMPA), leading to further Ca2+ influx. High intracellular Ca2+ activates various enzymes, including proteases (calpains), phospholipases, and endonucleases.
- **Mitochondrial Dysfunction:** Impaired oxidative phosphorylation leads to decreased ATP production and increased reactive oxygen species (ROS) generation. ROS cause oxidative damage to lipids, proteins, and DNA. Mitochondrial permeability transition pore (mPTP) opening can occur, leading to mitochondrial swelling, release of pro-apoptotic factors (like cytochrome c), and further cell death.
- **Caspase Activation:** The intrinsic (mitochondrial) pathway of apoptosis is activated. Cytochrome c release from mitochondria binds to Apaf-1, forming the apoptosome, which activates caspase-9. Caspase-9 then activates effector caspases (like caspase-3), which cleave cellular proteins, leading to the characteristic morphological changes of apoptosis (cell shrinkage, chromatin condensation/pyknosis, nuclear fragmentation/karyorrhexis, apoptotic bodies). While necrosis (coagulative in the brain) is the predominant pattern initially, apoptotic pathways are also significantly involved in neuronal death following ischemia.
- **Inflammation:** Microglia become activated and proliferate at the infarct border, releasing inflammatory mediators. Neutrophils infiltrate later (around 3-5 days), followed by macrophages (around 7 days), which clear debris.
- **Timeline:** The biopsy is performed 7 days post-stroke. By this time, the initial ischemic injury has progressed, leading to significant neuronal death. The described histological features (necrosis, pyknosis) are evident. The underlying mechanisms involve both necrosis (due to severe energy failure and membrane damage) and apoptosis (mediated by caspase activation triggered by mitochondrial dysfunction and excitotoxicity).

Therefore, neuronal death mediated by caspase activation following mitochondrial dysfunction and excitotoxicity is a central mechanism in ischemic stroke pathology.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Synaptic depression and decreased neuronal excitability due to impaired glutamate reuptake occur *early* in ischemia, contributing to the penumbra. However, the question asks about the mechanism of *neuronal death* observed 7 days later, which involves more severe and irreversible damage pathways like apoptosis and necrosis, not just synaptic depression. While glutamate reuptake impairment is part of the initial cascade, it's not the primary mechanism of cell death seen histologically at day 7.
- (B): Glial scar formation is a *later* event in the healing process following brain injury, typically occurring weeks to months after the initial insult. It involves proliferation and hypertrophy of astrocytes, forming a barrier. While astrocytes are involved in the response to injury, the primary mechanism of neuronal death at 7 days is not glial scar formation itself.
- (C): Liquefactive necrosis is characterized by enzymatic digestion of dead cells, resulting in a liquid mass. This is typical of bacterial or fungal infections or certain types of tumors in the brain, not the typical pattern of ischemic stroke, which is coagulative necrosis (at least initially). While liquefaction can occur later due to inflammation and enzymatic breakdown, the biopsy description (eosinophilic cytoplasm, pyknosis) points towards coagulative necrosis/apoptosis at day 7. Also, neutrophils are more prominent earlier (3-5 days), while macrophages are more prominent at 7 days, but the primary mechanism isn't just phagocytosis by these cells.
- (E): Demyelination is the loss of the myelin sheath surrounding axons. While some demyelination can occur secondary to ischemia (e.g., white matter involvement), the primary target of ischemic injury is the neurons themselves. The biopsy description focuses on neuronal and glial cell death (necrosis, pyknosis), not primarily demyelination with preserved axons and neuronal cell bodies.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                     | Ischemic Stroke (Neuronal Death) | Synaptic Depression (Early Ischemia) | Glial Scar Formation | Liquefactive Necrosis | Demyelination |
| :-------------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :------------------ | :-------------------- | :------------ |
| **Primary Cell Type Affected** | Neurons, Glial Cells              | Synapses, Neurons (functional)       | Astrocytes         | Various Cells         | Oligodendrocytes/Schwann Cells |
| **Mechanism**                | Excitotoxicity, Apoptosis, Necrosis | Impaired Glutamate Reuptake         | Astrocyte Proliferation | Enzymatic Digestion   | Myelin Breakdown |
| **Key Molecules/Pathways**   | Caspases, Mitochondria, Ca2+      | Glutamate Transporters               | GFAP, ECM           | Enzymes, Inflammation | Myelin Basic Protein |
| **Timeline**                 | Hours to Days                     | Minutes to Hours                     | Weeks to Months     | Variable              | Variable      |
| **Histology**                | Coagulative Necrosis, Pyknosis    | Functional Changes (not structural)  | Astrocytic Barrier  | Liquid Mass           | Axons Preserved |
| **Relevance to Q stem**      | **Correct**                       | Incorrect (too early/mechanism)      | Incorrect (too late) | Incorrect (wrong type) | Incorrect (wrong target) |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Ischemic stroke leads to neuronal death via complex pathways involving excitotoxicity, mitochondrial dysfunction, oxidative stress, and activation of apoptotic caspases. Histologically, this manifests initially as coagulative necrosis (eosinophilic cytoplasm, pyknosis) followed by inflammation and eventual glial scar formation. Differentiate this from liquefactive necrosis (seen in abscesses), demyelination (seen in MS), and early synaptic dysfunction. Remember the timeline of pathological changes post-stroke.

<a id='question-130'></a>

### Question 130: Central Nervous System Pathology: Focus 03-30 ((15% of all stroke cases))
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathophysiology, clinical presentation, and diagnostic imaging findings of lacunar infarcts, a specific type of ischemic stroke caused by occlusion of small penetrating arteries, often associated with chronic hypertension and diabetes mellitus. (考察脑梗死中腔隙性梗死病理生理学、临床表现和影像学发现的理解，这是一种由小穿透动脉闭塞引起的缺血性卒中，通常与慢性高血压和糖尿病有关。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a 20-pack-year smoking history and poorly controlled hypertension (average BP 160/95 mmHg) presents to the emergency department with acute onset of right-sided weakness and numbness affecting his face, arm, and leg. The symptoms began approximately 3 hours ago while he was watching television. His speech is slightly slurred, but he is otherwise alert and oriented. Neurological examination reveals moderate right hemiparesis (strength 3/5 in the upper and lower extremities), right hemisensory loss, and mild dysarthria. There are no signs of cranial nerve deficits other than mild facial droop on the right. Vital signs are stable. A non-contrast head CT scan performed immediately shows no evidence of hemorrhage. MRI of the brain performed 24 hours later reveals a small (<1.5 cm), well-defined lesion in the left internal capsule. There is no evidence of mass effect or surrounding edema.

Which of the following pathological findings is most likely present in the affected area?

(A) Liquefactive necrosis with neutrophilic infiltration and cystic cavity formation.
(B) Coagulative necrosis with preservation of cellular outlines and eosinophilic cytoplasm.
(C) Petechial hemorrhages and microglial nodules surrounding a central area of neuronal loss.
(D) Cystic encephalomalacia with gliosis and hemosiderin deposition.
(E) Spongiform degeneration with neuronal loss and astrocytic gliosis.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male with risk factors for cerebrovascular disease (smoking, hypertension).
- **Presentation:** Acute onset of focal neurological deficits (right hemiparesis, hemisensory loss, dysarthria) consistent with a stroke.
- **Timeline:** Symptoms started 3 hours ago, placing him within the window for potential thrombolysis (though not directly asked).
- **Initial Imaging (CT):** Non-contrast CT is negative for hemorrhage, ruling out hemorrhagic stroke. This is standard initial imaging for acute stroke.
- **Follow-up Imaging (MRI):** MRI reveals a small (<1.5 cm), well-defined lesion in the left internal capsule. This location (internal capsule) is characteristic of lacunar infarcts. The size (<1.5 cm) and lack of significant edema also fit the description of a lacunar infarct.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the most likely pathological finding in the affected area (the lacunar infarct).

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Lacunar Infarcts:** These are small (<1.5 cm) infarcts resulting from occlusion of small, penetrating arteries (e.g., lenticulostriate arteries arising from the MCA, thalamoperforating arteries arising from the PCA, pontine arteries arising from the basilar artery).
- **Etiology:** Most commonly caused by lipohyalinosis or microatheroma of these small vessels, strongly associated with chronic hypertension and diabetes mellitus.
- **Pathology:** The initial event is ischemic cell death (necrosis). In the brain, this typically manifests as coagulative necrosis initially, but the subsequent inflammatory response and tissue breakdown lead to liquefaction. Over time (days to weeks), the infarcted tissue is removed by macrophages, leading to the formation of a cystic cavity lined by reactive astrocytes (gliosis). Hemosiderin deposition occurs due to breakdown of red blood cells that extravasated during the initial ischemic event or subsequent minor hemorrhages. This end-stage appearance is termed cystic encephalomalacia.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The location of the infarct (internal capsule) explains the patient's symptoms (contralateral hemiparesis, hemisensory loss). The small size and lack of edema are typical for lacunar infarcts, which often present with pure motor, pure sensory, sensorimotor, or ataxic syndromes.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Liquefactive necrosis with neutrophilic infiltration and cystic cavity formation.** Liquefactive necrosis is characteristic of bacterial or fungal infections (abscesses) or ischemic injury in the brain (where the tissue lacks a fibrous stroma to maintain structure during necrosis). While liquefaction *eventually* occurs in infarcts, the *initial* necrosis is coagulative, and neutrophilic infiltration is more typical of infection than a sterile infarct, especially not the *defining* feature of a lacunar infarct's end stage. Cystic cavity formation is part of the end stage, but the description is incomplete and misleading regarding the initial necrosis type and inflammatory cells.
- (B) **Coagulative necrosis with preservation of cellular outlines and eosinophilic cytoplasm.** Coagulative necrosis is the typical pattern of necrosis seen in solid organs (like the heart or kidney) following ischemia. While it is the *initial* type of necrosis in brain infarcts, the question asks for the likely finding in the *affected area* described (a small, well-defined lesion on MRI 24 hours later), which implies a more evolved stage. Furthermore, the characteristic end-stage finding in lacunar infarcts is cystic encephalomalacia, not simply coagulative necrosis.
- (C) **Petechial hemorrhages and microglial nodules surrounding a central area of neuronal loss.** Petechial hemorrhages can occur in the acute phase of ischemic stroke, particularly in the cortex or cerebellum, but are less common in deep structures like the internal capsule associated with lacunar infarcts. Microglial nodules are a sign of chronic inflammation and are seen in various CNS conditions, including chronic infections, demyelination, and sometimes around old infarcts, but they are not the defining pathological feature of a lacunar infarct.
- (E) **Spongiform degeneration with neuronal loss and astrocytic gliosis.** Spongiform degeneration is characteristic of prion diseases (like Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease), characterized by widespread neuronal loss, vacuolation (spongiosis), and astrogliosis. This is completely unrelated to ischemic stroke or lacunar infarcts.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Lacunar Infarct (Correct Answer) | Abscess (Distractor A) | Acute Ischemic Stroke (Distractor B) | Hemorrhagic Stroke (Not an option, but relevant) | Prion Disease (Distractor E) |
|----------------------|-----------------------------------|------------------------|---------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|------------------------------|
| **Cause**            | Small vessel occlusion (HTN, DM)  | Bacterial/Fungal Infxn| Large/Small vessel occlusion          | Vessel rupture                                  | Prion accumulation           |
| **Size**             | <1.5 cm                          | Variable, often larger | Variable                               | Variable                                        | Diffuse                      |
| **Location**         | Deep structures (IC, basal ganglia, thalamus, pons) | Variable                  | Variable                               | Variable                                        | Diffuse                      |
| **Initial Necrosis** | Coagulative                      | Liquefactive           | Coagulative                            | N/A (Hemorrhage)                                 | N/A                          |
| **End-Stage Pathology**| Cystic encephalomalacia (cavity, gliosis, hemosiderin) | Liquefaction, pus, abscess wall | Variable (gliosis, encephalomalacia)           | Hematoma, hemosiderin         | Spongiosis, gliosis          |
| **Inflammation**     | Minimal (macrophages)             | Neutrophils, abscess wall | Minimal (microglia)                   | Minimal (unless secondary infection)            | Microglia, lymphoplasmacytic |
| **Imaging (MRI)**    | Small, well-defined, no edema     | Ring enhancement, edema | Variable (edema, restricted diffusion) | Blood products (T1/T2 hyperintensity)          | Cortical ribboning, basal ganglia hyperintensity |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Lacunar infarcts are small (<1.5 cm) ischemic strokes affecting deep brain structures, typically caused by small vessel disease related to hypertension and diabetes. The characteristic end-stage pathological finding is cystic encephalomalacia, characterized by a fluid-filled cavity, surrounding gliosis, and hemosiderin deposition. Differentiating lacunar infarcts from other stroke types (e.g., large vessel territory infarcts) or other CNS lesions (e.g., abscesses, tumors) relies on size, location, imaging characteristics, and clinical presentation.

<a id='question-131'></a>

### Question 131: Central Nervous System Pathology: Infarction
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathological changes, cellular mechanisms, and timing of neuronal injury and necrosis following cerebral infarction, specifically liquefactive necrosis and the role of inflammatory cells. (理解脑梗塞后的病理变化、细胞机制和时间进程，特别是坏液性坏死和炎症细胞的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of atrial fibrillation (on warfarin, INR 2.8), hypertension, and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute onset left-sided weakness and slurred speech, starting 3 hours prior to arrival. NIH Stroke Scale (NIHSS) score is 18. Non-contrast head CT performed immediately shows no evidence of hemorrhage. The patient is treated with intravenous alteplase (tPA) and admitted to the stroke unit. Serial neurological examinations over the next 72 hours reveal worsening of the initial deficits, followed by a period of relative stability. On hospital day 4, the patient develops signs of increased intracranial pressure (ICP), including headache, nausea, vomiting, and altered mental status. A repeat head CT scan reveals a large, well-defined area of low density in the right middle cerebral artery (MCA) territory, with significant surrounding edema and mass effect, including effacement of the sulci and midline shift. Microscopic examination of a biopsy taken from the infarcted area 5 days post-stroke would most likely show which of the following?

(A) Coagulative necrosis with preserved cellular outlines and eosinophilic cytoplasm.
(B) Caseous necrosis characterized by granulomas and Langhans giant cells.
(C) Liquefactive necrosis with neuronal cell bodies dissolving into a viscous, protein-rich fluid, surrounded by inflammatory cells.
(D) Fat necrosis with saponification and calcium deposition.
(E) Fibrinoid necrosis involving vessel walls, characteristic of vasculitis.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male with risk factors for stroke (atrial fibrillation, hypertension, hyperlipidemia).
*   **Presentation:** Acute onset focal neurological deficits (left-sided weakness, slurred speech) consistent with stroke.
*   **Initial Imaging:** Non-contrast CT negative for hemorrhage, ruling out hemorrhagic stroke.
*   **Treatment:** tPA administration, indicating ischemic stroke.
*   **Clinical Course:** Initial worsening followed by stability, then development of signs of increased ICP (headache, nausea, vomiting, altered mental status) on day 4.
*   **Repeat Imaging:** CT shows a large low-density area (infarct) in the right MCA territory with significant edema and mass effect. This indicates a large ischemic stroke with significant secondary injury (edema).
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the microscopic findings in a biopsy taken 5 days post-stroke. This timeframe is crucial for understanding the stage of infarction pathology.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Cerebral Ischemia:** Lack of blood flow leads to oxygen and glucose deprivation.
*   **Early Changes (0-12 hours):** Neuronal dysfunction, ion pump failure, cytotoxic edema (cellular swelling).
*   **Early Necrosis (12-24 hours):** Irreversible neuronal injury, release of excitatory amino acids (glutamate), calcium influx, activation of proteases and endonucleases, leading to cell death.
*   **Inflammation (24-72 hours):** Neutrophils infiltrate the infarcted area, attracted by chemokines and ischemia-modified proteins. They phagocytose cellular debris and contribute to further damage.
*   **Liquefactive Necrosis (3-5 days):** The hallmark of cerebral infarction. Neurons are rich in lipids and hydrolytic enzymes. After cell death, these enzymes digest the tissue, leading to liquefaction – the dead cells dissolve into a viscous, protein-rich fluid. This process is facilitated by the inflammatory response, particularly the activity of neutrophils and macrophages.
*   **Glial Response (1 week onwards):** Astrocytes proliferate at the periphery of the infarct, forming a glial scar. Microglia also play a role in phagocytosis and inflammation.
*   **Timeline:** The question asks about findings at 5 days post-stroke. This falls squarely within the period of established liquefactive necrosis and active inflammation.
*   **Why Liquefactive Necrosis?** Brain tissue lacks a substantial collagen framework, which is characteristic of tissues undergoing coagulative necrosis (like myocardial infarction). The high lipid content and enzymatic activity promote liquefaction.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Coagulative necrosis:** This is the typical pattern seen in solid organs like the heart, kidney, or spleen following ischemia. It involves protein denaturation, preservation of tissue architecture (ghost outlines of cells), and eosinophilic cytoplasm. It does *not* occur in the brain due to the brain's unique composition.
*   **(B) Caseous necrosis:** This is characteristic of tuberculosis and some fungal infections. It involves a "cheese-like" appearance, with amorphous granular debris surrounded by a granulomatous inflammatory reaction (lymphocytes, macrophages, Langhans giant cells). It is not associated with ischemic stroke.
*   **(D) Fat necrosis:** This occurs in adipose tissue, typically due to the release of pancreatic lipases (e.g., in acute pancreatitis) or trauma. It involves the breakdown of triglycerides into fatty acids, which combine with calcium to form chalky white deposits (saponification). It is irrelevant to cerebral infarction.
*   **(E) Fibrinoid necrosis:** This is typically seen in immune reactions involving blood vessels, such as vasculitis or malignant hypertension. It involves deposition of fibrin-like material in the vessel walls, leading to vessel damage. While stroke can be caused by vasculitis, the *necrosis within the brain parenchyma* following ischemic stroke is liquefactive, not fibrinoid. Fibrinoid necrosis affects the *vessels*, not the brain tissue itself in this context.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Cerebral Infarction (Ischemic Stroke) | Myocardial Infarction | Tuberculosis | Acute Pancreatitis | Vasculitis |
| :------------------ | :------------------------------------ | :-------------------- | :----------- | :----------------- | :--------- |
| **Necrosis Type**   | Liquefactive                          | Coagulative           | Caseous      | Fat                | Fibrinoid  |
| **Tissue**          | Brain                                 | Heart                 | Lung         | Adipose Tissue     | Blood Vessels |
| **Cellular Outlines** | Lost early                            | Preserved initially   | Lost         | N/A                | N/A        |
| **Inflammation**    | Neutrophils, Macrophages (days)       | Neutrophils (days)    | Granulomas   | Lipase-induced     | Lymphocytes, Macrophages |
| **Key Feature**     | Liquefaction, viscous fluid            | Ghost cells           | Granulomas   | Saponification     | Fibrin Deposition |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Cerebral infarction characteristically results in liquefactive necrosis due to the high lipid content and enzymatic activity of brain tissue, leading to the dissolution of dead cells into a protein-rich fluid. This contrasts sharply with coagulative necrosis seen in other ischemic tissues like the heart. Remember the timeline: liquefaction becomes prominent around 3-5 days post-infarction. Key differentials for necrosis types include coagulative (heart), caseous (TB), fat (pancreatitis), and fibrinoid (vasculitis).

<a id='question-132'></a>

### Question 132: Central Nervous System Pathology: Stroke Etiology & Pathogenesis
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating ischemic stroke subtypes (thrombotic vs. embolic vs. lacunar) based on clinical presentation, imaging findings, and underlying pathology (atherosclerosis, cardioembolism, small vessel disease). 梗塞性脑卒中的亚型鉴别 (血栓性 vs 栓塞性 vs 小血管病) 基于临床表现、影像学发现和潜在病理 (动脉粥样硬化、心源性栓塞、小血管病).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of poorly controlled hypertension, hyperlipidemia, and type 2 diabetes mellitus presents to the emergency department with acute onset of right-sided hemiparesis and aphasia. His symptoms began 3 hours prior to arrival. Vital signs show a blood pressure of 180/105 mmHg. Neurological examination reveals dense right hemiplegia (strength 0/5 in the right upper and lower extremities), global aphasia, and right facial droop. A non-contrast head CT scan performed immediately upon arrival is negative for hemorrhage. CT angiography (CTA) reveals a complete occlusion of the left middle cerebral artery (MCA) proximal to the M1 segment. The patient is immediately started on intravenous alteplase (tPA). Twenty-four hours later, a follow-up MRI shows a well-defined area of restricted diffusion in the left MCA territory, consistent with acute infarction. Histopathological examination of brain tissue obtained via biopsy 3 days post-stroke reveals a pale, swollen area with loss of cellular detail and neuronal necrosis, surrounded by a rim of gliosis. Microscopic examination shows liquefactive necrosis with phagocytosis of cellular debris by macrophages. There is no evidence of hemorrhage or vasculitis.

Which of the following pathological findings is MOST likely responsible for the patient's acute ischemic stroke?

(A) Fibrinoid necrosis of small penetrating arteries with microatheroma formation.
(B) Mural thrombus formation overlying an atherosclerotic plaque in a large extracranial artery.
(C) Embolic occlusion of a large cerebral artery by a thrombus originating from the left atrium.
(D) Hyaline arteriolosclerosis with occlusion of small perforating arteries in the basal ganglia.
(E) Septic emboli from infective endocarditis causing multiple small, scattered infarcts.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male with multiple vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia, diabetes). This increases the likelihood of atherosclerosis and thromboembolic events.
*   **Acute Presentation:** Sudden onset right hemiparesis and aphasia, localizing to the left cerebral hemisphere, specifically the MCA territory. This is a classic stroke presentation.
*   **Time Course:** Symptoms began 3 hours prior, placing the patient within the window for thrombolysis (tPA).
*   **Imaging:** Non-contrast CT negative for hemorrhage rules out hemorrhagic stroke. CTA shows a complete occlusion of the *left MCA proximal to the M1 segment*. This is a critical finding indicating a large vessel occlusion (LVO).
*   **Treatment:** Administration of tPA suggests a diagnosis of acute ischemic stroke.
*   **MRI:** Follow-up MRI confirms acute infarction in the left MCA territory.
*   **Histopathology:** Describes typical features of ischemic stroke: pale, swollen tissue, loss of cellular detail, neuronal necrosis, liquefactive necrosis, and phagocytosis by macrophages. This confirms the diagnosis of ischemic stroke.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most likely pathological finding responsible* for the stroke. Given the clinical presentation (LVO in MCA territory) and risk factors, we need to determine the most probable underlying cause.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Ischemic Stroke Pathogenesis:** Ischemic stroke results from inadequate blood flow to the brain, leading to neuronal injury and death. The underlying cause can be thrombotic (in situ clot formation, often on atherosclerotic plaque) or embolic (clot or other material travels from elsewhere).
*   **Large Vessel Occlusion (LVO):** Occlusion of major cerebral arteries like the MCA typically causes extensive infarction in the territory supplied by that artery.
*   **Common Causes of LVO:**
    *   **Cardioembolism:** Thrombi originating from the heart (e.g., atrial fibrillation, valvular disease, post-MI mural thrombus) travel to the brain. This is a very common cause of LVO.
    *   **Large Artery Atherosclerosis:** Thrombosis occurs on an atherosclerotic plaque in the extracranial (e.g., carotid artery) or intracranial (e.g., MCA) arteries. This is also a common cause, especially in patients with significant vascular risk factors.
*   **Differential Diagnosis of Stroke Subtypes:**
    *   **Thrombotic Stroke:** Often associated with underlying atherosclerosis. Plaque rupture or erosion leads to thrombus formation *in situ*. Can occur in large or small vessels.
    *   **Embolic Stroke:** Caused by emboli traveling from a distant source (most commonly the heart). Often presents with sudden, maximal deficit at onset.
    *   **Lacunar Stroke:** Small infarcts (<1.5 cm) in the deep structures of the brain (basal ganglia, thalamus, pons, internal capsule) caused by occlusion of small penetrating arteries (lenticulostriate arteries). Typically associated with chronic hypertension and lipohyalinosis/microatheroma formation.
*   **Patient's Case Analysis:** The patient has significant vascular risk factors (HTN, HLD, DM), making both atherosclerosis and cardioembolism plausible. The CTA shows a *complete occlusion of the proximal left MCA*. While atherosclerosis can cause thrombosis in the MCA, cardioembolism is a very frequent cause of proximal MCA occlusion leading to large territorial infarcts. The sudden onset and maximal deficit are also characteristic of embolic events. The histopathology describes typical ischemic changes, not specific to the cause but consistent with infarction. Given the LVO in a major artery and the patient's risk profile, both atherosclerosis (leading to thrombosis) and cardioembolism are strong possibilities. However, embolic occlusion from the heart (often left atrium in atrial fibrillation, though not explicitly stated here) is a leading cause of such events.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Fibrinoid necrosis of small penetrating arteries with microatheroma formation. This describes the pathology of *lacunar infarcts*, which are typically small (<1.5 cm) and occur in deep brain structures, not large territorial infarcts like the one described (left MCA territory). The patient's symptoms (hemiplegia, aphasia) and imaging (large MCA territory infarct) are inconsistent with a lacunar stroke.
- (B): Mural thrombus formation overlying an atherosclerotic plaque in a *large extracranial artery*. While atherosclerosis is present (risk factors), the CTA showed occlusion of the *intracranial* MCA. Thrombosis can occur in the intracranial MCA itself, but the question asks for the *most likely* cause. Embolism from the heart is a very common cause of proximal MCA occlusion. Thrombosis *in situ* within the intracranial MCA is also possible due to atherosclerosis, but embolism is often considered equally or more likely, especially without specific mention of carotid stenosis. Option (C) is more specific to the likely source of the occlusion given the LVO.
- (D): Hyaline arteriolosclerosis with occlusion of small perforating arteries in the basal ganglia. This is the pathology associated with *lacunar infarcts*, similar to (A). It involves small vessels and deep structures, inconsistent with the patient's large MCA territory infarct.
- (E): Septic emboli from infective endocarditis causing multiple small, scattered infarcts. Septic emboli typically cause multiple, smaller infarcts, often associated with systemic signs of infection (fever, new murmur). The patient has a single, large infarct in a specific vascular territory (MCA), and there's no mention of signs of endocarditis.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | (A) Lacunar Infarct (Small Vessel) | (B) Thrombotic Stroke (Large Artery) | (C) Embolic Stroke (Large Artery) | (D) Lacunar Infarct (Small Vessel) | (E) Septic Emboli |
| :------------------ | :--------------------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :--------------------------------- | :--------------------------------- | :---------------- |
| **Vessel Size**     | Small penetrating arteries         | Large arteries (extracranial/intracranial) | Large arteries (extracranial/intracranial) | Small penetrating arteries         | Variable          |
| **Infarct Size**    | Small (<1.5 cm)                    | Large (territorial)                  | Large (territorial)                  | Small (<1.5 cm)                    | Small, multiple   |
| **Location**        | Deep structures (basal ganglia, etc.) | Cortical/Subcortical (territorial)   | Cortical/Subcortical (territorial)   | Deep structures (basal ganglia, etc.) | Scattered         |
| **Pathology**       | Lipohyalinosis, microatheroma, fibrinoid necrosis | Atherosclerosis, mural thrombus       | Thrombus/Embolus (often cardiac)   | Hyaline arteriolosclerosis, microatheroma | Vegetation fragments |
| **Clinical Picture** | Pure motor/sensory deficits, etc. | Hemiplegia, aphasia, etc.            | Hemiplegia, aphasia, etc.            | Pure motor/sensory deficits, etc. | Fever, multiple deficits |
| **Patient Case Fit** | Poor                               | Possible                             | **Most Likely**                     | Poor                               | Poor              |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Ischemic stroke can be caused by thrombosis (in situ clot formation, often on atherosclerotic plaque) or embolism (clot/material traveling from elsewhere). Large vessel occlusions (like MCA) causing large territorial infarcts are commonly due to cardioembolism or thrombosis related to atherosclerosis. Lacunar infarcts are small, deep infarcts caused by small vessel disease (lipohyalinosis, microatheroma) typically associated with chronic hypertension. Differentiating stroke subtypes based on clinical presentation, imaging, and risk factors is crucial. Key differentials include thrombotic vs. embolic stroke, and large vessel vs. small vessel (lacunar) stroke.

<a id='question-133'></a>

### Question 133: Central Nervous System Pathology: Focus 03-33 (• Types:)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathological features, molecular mechanisms, and clinical presentation of Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease (CJD), a prion disease, specifically focusing on the characteristic spongiform changes and the nature of the infectious agent. (考点：克雅氏-布氏病 (CJD) 的病理特征、分子机制和临床表现，特别是海绵状变和传染原的性质。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department with rapidly progressive dementia, ataxia, and myoclonus over the past 6 months. His wife reports that he initially had subtle memory problems but quickly deteriorated, developing difficulty walking and involuntary muscle jerks. Neurological examination reveals severe cognitive impairment, generalized hyperreflexia, and prominent myoclonus. MRI of the brain shows diffuse cortical ribboning and hyperintensity in the basal ganglia and thalamus on DWI (diffusion-weighted imaging) sequences. Cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) analysis shows normal cell count and protein levels but is positive for 14-3-3 protein. A brain biopsy is performed, revealing neuronal loss, astrogliosis, and prominent spongiform changes characterized by numerous small, clear vacuoles within the neuropil, without significant inflammation. Immunohistochemical staining for prion protein (PrP) shows diffuse synaptic and glial deposition of an abnormal isoform. Which of the following best describes the underlying pathological process?

(A) Formation of amyloid plaques composed of misfolded beta-amyloid protein, leading to neuronal dysfunction and death.
(B) Accumulation of hyperphosphorylated tau protein forming neurofibrillary tangles, disrupting microtubule function and axonal transport.
(C) Conversion of the normal cellular prion protein (PrP^C) into a misfolded, infectious isoform (PrP^Sc), leading to neuronal damage and spongiform degeneration.
(D) Deposition of alpha-synuclein aggregates forming Lewy bodies, primarily affecting dopaminergic neurons in the substantia nigra and leading to motor symptoms.
(E) Accumulation of TDP-43 protein aggregates in the cytoplasm of neurons and glial cells, associated with frontotemporal lobar degeneration.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with rapidly progressive dementia, ataxia, and myoclonus over 6 months. This rapid progression and triad of symptoms are highly suggestive of CJD.
- **Neurological Examination:** Severe cognitive impairment, hyperreflexia, prominent myoclonus. These findings are classic for CJD.
- **MRI Findings:** Diffuse cortical ribboning and hyperintensity in basal ganglia/thalamus on DWI. These are characteristic MRI findings in sporadic CJD (sCJD). DWI hyperintensity reflects cytotoxic edema associated with neuronal damage.
- **CSF Analysis:** Normal cell count/protein, positive 14-3-3 protein. Elevated 14-3-3 protein in CSF is a supportive marker for CJD, although not entirely specific.
- **Brain Biopsy:** Neuronal loss, astrogliosis, prominent spongiform changes (small clear vacuoles in neuropil), diffuse synaptic/glial deposition of abnormal PrP (PrP^Sc) on immunohistochemistry. These are the definitive pathological hallmarks of prion diseases like CJD. The absence of significant inflammation helps differentiate it from infectious encephalitis.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying pathological process.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **CJD Pathogenesis:** CJD is caused by prions, which are infectious agents composed primarily of the misfolded prion protein (PrP^Sc). The normal cellular prion protein (PrP^C) is a glycosylphosphatidylinositol (GPI)-anchored glycoprotein found predominantly on the surface of neurons. PrP^Sc acts as a template, inducing the conformational change of PrP^C into the abnormal PrP^Sc form. This conversion process leads to the accumulation of PrP^Sc aggregates, which are toxic to neurons.
- **Spongiform Degeneration:** The accumulation of PrP^Sc causes neuronal loss and the formation of characteristic spongiform changes – small, clear vacuoles within the neuropil (neuronal processes and dendrites). This gives the brain tissue a sponge-like appearance.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The rapid accumulation of PrP^Sc and subsequent neuronal damage explains the rapid progression of dementia, ataxia, and myoclonus seen in CJD.
- **Diagnostic Markers:** MRI findings (DWI hyperintensity) and CSF markers (14-3-3 protein, RT-QuIC assay) support the diagnosis, but brain biopsy showing spongiform changes and PrP^Sc deposition is considered the gold standard.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Amyloid plaques (beta-amyloid)** are characteristic of Alzheimer's disease. While Alzheimer's causes dementia, it typically has a much slower progression (years), and the pathological hallmarks are plaques and neurofibrillary tangles, not spongiform changes or PrP^Sc deposition. The MRI findings are also different (atrophy, not DWI hyperintensity).
- (B): **Neurofibrillary tangles (hyperphosphorylated tau)** are also characteristic of Alzheimer's disease and other tauopathies. Tau pathology disrupts axonal transport. Again, the clinical course, MRI, and specific pathology (spongiform changes, PrP^Sc) do not fit.
- (D): **Lewy bodies (alpha-synuclein)** are characteristic of Parkinson's disease and Dementia with Lewy Bodies. These diseases typically present with motor symptoms (Parkinsonism) and cognitive decline, but the pathology involves alpha-synuclein aggregates, not spongiform changes or PrP^Sc. The MRI findings are also different.
- (E): **TDP-43 aggregates** are associated with frontotemporal lobar degeneration (FTLD) and amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS). FTLD typically presents with behavioral changes or language deficits, and the pathology involves TDP-43 inclusions, not spongiform changes or PrP^Sc.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Creutzfeldt-Jakob Disease (CJD) | Alzheimer's Disease (AD) | Parkinson's Disease (PD) / Lewy Body Dementia (LBD) | Frontotemporal Lobar Degeneration (FTLD) |
|----------------------|-----------------------------------|---------------------------|------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------|
| **Primary Pathology** | PrP^Sc deposition, Spongiform change | Amyloid plaques, Tau tangles | Lewy bodies (alpha-synuclein)                       | TDP-43 or FUS aggregates                  |
| **Clinical Course**   | Rapidly progressive dementia, ataxia, myoclonus | Slow progressive dementia | Parkinsonism, cognitive decline (LBD)              | Behavioral/language changes, motor neuron signs (FTD/ALS) |
| **MRI**              | DWI hyperintensity (basal ganglia, cortex) | Atrophy                  | Variable, may show atrophy                          | Frontal/temporal atrophy                  |
| **CSF**              | 14-3-3 protein (+), RT-QuIC (+)    | Amyloid/Tau levels (variable) | Alpha-synuclein (variable)                          | TDP-43 levels (variable)                  |
| **Key Protein**      | PrP^Sc                            | Beta-amyloid, Tau         | Alpha-synuclein                                       | TDP-43, FUS                               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
CJD is a rapidly progressive neurodegenerative disease caused by misfolded prion protein (PrP^Sc). The key pathological features are spongiform changes in the brain parenchyma and the deposition of PrP^Sc. Clinically, it presents with rapidly progressive dementia, ataxia, and myoclonus. MRI often shows characteristic DWI hyperintensities. Differentiating CJD from other dementias like Alzheimer's (plaques, tangles, slow progression), Parkinson's/LBD (Lewy bodies, motor symptoms), and FTLD (TDP-43, behavioral changes) relies on the specific clinical presentation, MRI findings, and ultimately, pathological examination.
