<a id='question-141'></a>

### Question 141: Central Nervous System Pathology: Focus 03-41 (cerebral artery territory.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the specific vascular territories supplied by the major cerebral arteries (anterior, middle, posterior) and the resulting clinical syndromes when these territories are affected by ischemia or infarction. (理解大脑主要动脉（前、中、后）供应的特定区域以及这些区域受缺血或梗塞影响时产生的临床综合征。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of atrial fibrillation (on warfarin, INR 2.8), hypertension, and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute onset of right-sided weakness and sensory loss. The weakness predominantly affects the face and arm, with less involvement of the leg. He also exhibits difficulty speaking, characterized by non-fluent speech and impaired comprehension (Broca's aphasia). Ocular examination reveals right homonymous hemianopia. Neurological examination shows right facial droop, right arm drift, and decreased sensation on the right side of the body. Reflexes are normal bilaterally, and plantar responses are flexor. A non-contrast head CT scan shows a well-defined hypodensity in the left parietal lobe extending into the frontal lobe, sparing the occipital lobe.

Which of the following cerebral arteries is most likely occluded, leading to this patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Left anterior cerebral artery (ACA)
(B) Left middle cerebral artery (MCA)
(C) Left posterior cerebral artery (PCA)
(D) Right anterior cerebral artery (ACA)
(E) Right middle cerebral artery (MCA)

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male with risk factors for stroke (atrial fibrillation, hypertension, hyperlipidemia). Atrial fibrillation is a major source of cardioembolic stroke.
*   **Acute Onset:** Suggests a vascular event (stroke).
*   **Right-sided Weakness and Sensory Loss:** Indicates a lesion in the contralateral (left) cerebral hemisphere affecting the motor and sensory cortex or their descending pathways.
*   **Face and Arm > Leg Weakness:** This pattern is characteristic of involvement of the lateral aspect of the motor cortex, which controls the face, arm, and trunk, and is primarily supplied by the MCA. The leg area is more medial and supplied by the ACA.
*   **Non-fluent Speech (Broca's Aphasia) and Impaired Comprehension:** Broca's area (motor speech) is typically located in the inferior frontal gyrus, and Wernicke's area (language comprehension) is in the superior temporal gyrus. Both are predominantly supplied by the MCA.
*   **Right Homonymous Hemianopia:** This visual field defect indicates a lesion affecting the optic radiations or visual cortex. The optic radiations pass through the temporal and parietal lobes, and the visual cortex is in the occipital lobe. The MCA supplies parts of the temporal and parietal lobes, including the optic radiations. The PCA supplies the occipital lobe. The presence of hemianopia *without* occipital lobe involvement on CT suggests damage to the optic radiations in the temporal/parietal lobe.
*   **CT Findings:** Hypodensity in the left parietal lobe extending into the frontal lobe, sparing the occipital lobe. This location corresponds to the territory supplied by the left MCA, including the motor cortex (frontal lobe), sensory cortex (parietal lobe), and parts of the temporal lobe (involved in language and optic radiations). The sparing of the occipital lobe argues against PCA involvement as the primary cause of the visual field defect.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Cerebral Arterial Territories:** The brain is divided into territories supplied by specific arteries originating from the Circle of Willis.
    *   **Anterior Cerebral Artery (ACA):** Supplies the medial aspects of the frontal and parietal lobes, including the motor and sensory cortex for the contralateral leg and foot, and parts of the basal ganglia and internal capsule. Occlusion typically causes contralateral leg weakness > arm/face weakness, urinary incontinence, and behavioral changes.
    *   **Middle Cerebral Artery (MCA):** The largest cerebral artery, supplying the lateral aspects of the frontal, parietal, and temporal lobes, including the motor and sensory cortex for the contralateral face, arm, and trunk, Broca's area, Wernicke's area, and parts of the basal ganglia and internal capsule. Occlusion typically causes contralateral hemiparesis (face/arm > leg), hemisensory loss, homonymous hemianopia (due to optic radiation involvement), and aphasia (if the dominant hemisphere is affected).
    *   **Posterior Cerebral Artery (PCA):** Supplies the occipital lobe (visual cortex), inferior temporal lobe, thalamus, and midbrain. Occlusion typically causes contralateral homonymous hemianopia (often macular sparing), visual agnosia, memory impairment, and contralateral hemiparesis (less common, due to thalamic/brainstem involvement).
*   **Patient's Syndrome:** The patient presents with contralateral (right) hemiparesis (face/arm > leg), contralateral hemisensory loss, Broca's aphasia, and right homonymous hemianopia. The CT scan shows infarction in the left parietal and frontal lobes. This constellation of findings perfectly matches the territory supplied by the left MCA.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Left anterior cerebral artery (ACA):** Occlusion would primarily affect the medial aspect of the left frontal and parietal lobes. This would cause contralateral (right) leg weakness and sensory loss, potentially urinary incontinence, and possibly some behavioral changes. It would *not* typically cause significant face/arm weakness, aphasia, or hemianopia involving the optic radiations in the parietal lobe. The CT findings are also inconsistent with ACA territory.
- (C) **Left posterior cerebral artery (PCA):** Occlusion would primarily affect the left occipital lobe and parts of the temporal lobe and thalamus. This would cause contralateral (right) homonymous hemianopia, potentially with macular sparing, and possibly memory deficits or visual agnosia. It would *not* typically cause significant motor or sensory deficits affecting the face/arm more than the leg, nor Broca's aphasia. The CT findings are inconsistent with PCA territory (occipital lobe spared).
- (D) **Right anterior cerebral artery (ACA):** Occlusion would affect the right hemisphere, causing contralateral (left) leg weakness/sensory loss. This is inconsistent with the patient's right-sided deficits.
- (E) **Right middle cerebral artery (MCA):** Occlusion would affect the right hemisphere, causing contralateral (left) hemiparesis (face/arm > leg), hemisensory loss, and potentially neglect syndrome (if non-dominant hemisphere) or global aphasia (if dominant hemisphere, but less common). It would *not* cause right-sided weakness, sensory loss, or right homonymous hemianopia.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Left ACA Occlusion                               | Left MCA Occlusion                                  | Left PCA Occlusion                                  | Right ACA Occlusion                             | Right MCA Occlusion                             |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------- |
| **Affected Hemisphere** | Left                                             | Left                                                | Left                                                | Right                                             | Right                                             |
| **Motor Deficit**       | Contralateral leg > arm/face                     | Contralateral face/arm > leg                        | Minimal/Contralateral hemiparesis (thalamic/brainstem) | Contralateral leg > arm/face                     | Contralateral face/arm > leg                        |
| **Sensory Deficit**     | Contralateral leg > arm/face                     | Contralateral face/arm > leg                        | Minimal/Contralateral hemisensory loss (thalamic)   | Contralateral leg > arm/face                     | Contralateral face/arm > leg                        |
| **Aphasia**             | No (unless bilateral or specific lesion)         | Yes (Broca's/Wernicke's/Global) if dominant hemisphere | No                                                  | No (unless bilateral or specific lesion)         | No (unless bilateral or specific lesion)         |
| **Visual Field Defect** | No                                               | Yes (Contralateral Homonymous Hemianopia)            | Yes (Contralateral Homonymous Hemianopia)            | No                                               | Yes (Contralateral Homonymous Hemianopia)            |
| **Other**               | Urinary incontinence, behavioral changes          | Neglect (if non-dominant), hemispatial neglect       | Visual agnosia, memory impairment                  | Urinary incontinence, behavioral changes          | Neglect (if non-dominant), hemispatial neglect       |
| **CT Location**         | Medial Frontal/Parietal Lobe                      | Lateral Frontal/Parietal/Temporal Lobe              | Occipital Lobe, Inferior Temporal Lobe, Thalamus    | Medial Frontal/Parietal Lobe                      | Lateral Frontal/Parietal/Temporal Lobe              |
| **Patient's Symptoms**  | No match                                         | **Excellent Match**                                 | No match                                             | No match                                         | No match                                         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The clinical presentation (contralateral face/arm > leg weakness, aphasia, hemisensory loss, homonymous hemianopia) and CT findings (lateral frontal/parietal infarct) are classic for a left MCA territory stroke. Differentiating stroke syndromes based on vascular territories is crucial for rapid diagnosis and management. Key differentials include ACA and PCA strokes, which present with distinct patterns of deficits.

<a id='question-142'></a>

### Question 142: Central Nervous System Pathology: Focus 03-42 (Can be due to thrombotic occlusion in the setting of)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathological mechanisms underlying ischemic stroke, specifically focusing on the cellular changes that occur in the brain tissue following a thrombotic occlusion, and the characteristic histological features observed in different time frames post-stroke. (本题考察缺血性脑卒中的病理机制，特别是血栓性闭塞后脑组织细胞的变化以及不同时间点观察到的组织学特征。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 72-year-old male with a history of hypertension, hyperlipidemia, and atrial fibrillation (on warfarin, INR 2.8) presents to the emergency department with acute onset of left-sided weakness and slurred speech, starting 3 hours prior to arrival. Neurological examination reveals right hemiparesis (strength 3/5 in the upper extremity, 4/5 in the lower extremity), right facial droop, and dysarthria. NIH Stroke Scale (NIHSS) score is 12. Non-contrast CT head performed immediately shows no evidence of hemorrhage. The patient is treated with intravenous alteplase (tPA) and admitted to the stroke unit. 24 hours after symptom onset, the patient's neurological deficits have not significantly improved. A repeat MRI scan reveals a well-defined area of restricted diffusion in the left middle cerebral artery (MCA) territory. A subsequent brain biopsy is performed 7 days after symptom onset from the infarcted region. Microscopic examination of the biopsy specimen shows a central area of neuronal necrosis with eosinophilic cytoplasm and pyknotic nuclei, surrounded by a zone of gliosis and infiltration by neutrophils and macrophages.

What is the most likely histological finding in the central area of infarction 7 days after the onset of symptoms?

(A) Mallory bodies within astrocytes
(B) Liquefactive necrosis with formation of a cystic cavity
(C) Neuronal necrosis with eosinophilic cytoplasm and pyknotic nuclei
(D) Perivascular lymphocytic cuffing and demyelination
(E) Amyloid plaques and neurofibrillary tangles

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 72-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia, atrial fibrillation) – high risk for stroke.
*   **Presentation:** Acute onset left-sided weakness, slurred speech (right hemiparesis, facial droop, dysarthria) – classic signs of a stroke affecting the right cerebral hemisphere, likely MCA territory.
*   **Timeline:** Symptoms started 3 hours ago, CT negative for hemorrhage, treated with tPA.
*   **Progression:** No significant improvement after 24 hours.
*   **Imaging:** MRI shows restricted diffusion in the left MCA territory – confirms acute ischemic stroke.
*   **Biopsy:** Performed 7 days after symptom onset.
*   **Histology:** Central area described as neuronal necrosis with eosinophilic cytoplasm and pyknotic nuclei, surrounded by inflammation (neutrophils, macrophages) and gliosis.
*   **Question:** Asks for the histological finding in the *central area* of infarction *7 days* after onset.

The key clues are the clinical presentation consistent with an ischemic stroke (likely thrombotic given the risk factors and lack of hemorrhage on initial CT), the confirmation by MRI, and the specific histological description obtained from a biopsy performed 7 days post-stroke. The question specifically asks about the *central* area of the infarct at this time point.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Ischemic Stroke Pathogenesis:** Thrombotic occlusion of a cerebral artery leads to cessation of blood flow, causing hypoxia and energy failure (ATP depletion). This disrupts ion homeostasis (influx of Na+, Ca2+; efflux of K+), leading to cytotoxic edema. Excitotoxicity (excessive glutamate release) further contributes to neuronal injury.
*   **Cellular Changes Post-Ischemia:**
    *   **Early (0-12 hours):** Reversible neuronal injury, early signs of necrosis.
    *   **Early-Intermediate (12-24 hours):** Irreversible neuronal injury, neuronal necrosis begins. Neurons become eosinophilic, nuclei pyknotic (shrink and condense). Edema increases. Neutrophils begin to infiltrate.
    *   **Intermediate (24-72 hours):** Peak of neuronal necrosis. Neutrophil infiltration is maximal. Breakdown of the blood-brain barrier.
    *   **Late (3-7 days):** Macrophages replace neutrophils. Liquefaction necrosis begins in the central core, leading to the formation of a cystic cavity. Gliosis (astrocytosis) starts at the periphery.
    *   **Chronic (>2 weeks):** Cystic cavity expands. Glial scar formation.
*   **Histological Features:**
    *   **Neuronal Necrosis:** Characterized by eosinophilic cytoplasm, pyknotic nuclei, and eventual karyorrhexis (nuclear fragmentation). This is the hallmark of cell death in the infarct core.
    *   **Liquefactive Necrosis:** Enzymatic digestion of dead cells, resulting in a liquid, viscous mass that eventually forms a cystic cavity. This typically occurs in the central core after several days (around 3-7 days).
    *   **Inflammation:** Neutrophils infiltrate early (within hours), followed by macrophages (starting around day 2-3).
    *   **Gliosis:** Reactive proliferation of astrocytes, forming a glial scar at the periphery of the infarct.

In this case, the biopsy was performed 7 days after symptom onset. The central area of the infarct would be undergoing liquefactive necrosis, forming a cystic cavity, and characterized by the remnants of necrotic neurons (eosinophilic cytoplasm, pyknotic nuclei) and inflammatory cells (macrophages). Option (C) describes the *initial* stage of neuronal necrosis, which is prominent in the first 24-72 hours but is still present as part of the necrotic tissue undergoing liquefaction at 7 days. However, the *most characteristic* finding in the *central* area at 7 days is the liquefaction process itself, which involves the breakdown of these necrotic cells. The description provided in the vignette ("neuronal necrosis with eosinophilic cytoplasm and pyknotic nuclei") is the *cellular hallmark* of the infarct, and while liquefaction is occurring, these necrotic cells are the substrate for it. Option (C) accurately describes the state of the dead neurons that are undergoing liquefaction. Option (B) describes the *result* of the liquefaction process (cystic cavity), which is also occurring at 7 days, but the cellular changes described in (C) are the fundamental process happening within the core. Given the options, (C) is the most direct description of the cellular pathology within the infarct core at this stage, representing the necrotic neurons that are being liquefied.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Mallory bodies** are eosinophilic cytoplasmic inclusions found in hepatocytes undergoing degenerative changes, typically associated with alcoholic liver disease or other chronic liver injuries. They are not found in brain tissue following ischemic stroke. This option is incorrect.
- (B): **Liquefactive necrosis with formation of a cystic cavity** is indeed a characteristic feature of ischemic stroke, particularly in the central core, and it becomes prominent around 3-7 days post-infarction. However, the vignette *also* describes the cellular features *within* the necrotic tissue (eosinophilic cytoplasm, pyknotic nuclei). While liquefaction is occurring, option (C) describes the *cellular* state of the infarct core at this time, which is the direct consequence of the initial ischemic injury and the precursor to liquefaction. Both (B) and (C) describe processes occurring at 7 days, but (C) describes the *cellular* pathology that *underlies* the liquefaction described in (B). The question asks for the histological finding, and the description provided in the vignette strongly points towards the cellular changes of necrosis. Therefore, (C) is a more precise description of the *cellular* hallmark observed microscopically, even as liquefaction is underway.
- (D): **Perivascular lymphocytic cuffing and demyelination** are characteristic features of inflammatory demyelinating diseases like multiple sclerosis, not ischemic stroke. This option is incorrect.
- (E): **Amyloid plaques and neurofibrillary tangles** are the pathological hallmarks of Alzheimer's disease, a neurodegenerative disorder. They are not features of acute or subacute ischemic stroke. This option is incorrect.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Ischemic Stroke (7 days)                                  | Mallory Bodies (Liver) | Liquefactive Necrosis (General) | Multiple Sclerosis (Demyelination) | Alzheimer's Disease (Plaques/Tangles) |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------- | :------------------------------ | :--------------------------------- | :------------------------------------ |
| **Cellular Hallmark**   | Neuronal necrosis (eosinophilic, pyknotic) -> Liquefaction | Hepatocyte degeneration | Cell death -> Liquefaction       | Demyelination, inflammation       | Amyloid plaques, neurofibrillary tangles |
| **Inflammation**        | Neutrophils (early), Macrophages (later)                 | Variable               | Neutrophils, Macrophages         | Lymphocytes (perivascular)         | Microglia, astrocytes                |
| **Tissue Appearance**   | Soft, liquefying infarct core                             | Liver tissue           | Variable (brain, abscess)       | White matter lesions              | Brain atrophy, plaques, tangles      |
| **Underlying Cause**    | Thrombotic/Embolic Occlusion                              | Alcohol, toxins        | Ischemia, infection             | Autoimmune attack                 | Protein misfolding, aging            |
| **Location**            | Brain (MCA territory in this case)                        | Liver                  | Brain, abscess, etc.            | CNS white matter                  | Brain cortex, hippocampus            |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key takeaway is understanding the temporal evolution of histological changes in ischemic stroke. Early changes (0-24h) involve reversible injury and early necrosis. Intermediate changes (24-72h) show peak neuronal necrosis and neutrophil infiltration. Late changes (3-7 days) involve liquefactive necrosis, macrophage infiltration, and the beginning of gliosis. Differentiating ischemic stroke pathology from other CNS conditions like demyelinating diseases (MS) or neurodegenerative diseases (Alzheimer's) is crucial. Remember that neuronal necrosis (eosinophilic cytoplasm, pyknotic nuclei) is the fundamental cellular event leading to liquefaction in the infarct core. (本题要点：理解缺血性脑卒中组织学变化的temporal evolution。早期变化（0-24h）涉及可逆损伤和早期坏死。中期变化（24-72h）显示神经元坏死高峰和中性粒细胞浸润。晚期变化（3-7天）涉及坏液性坏死、巨噬细胞浸润和胶质细胞增生开始。区分缺血性脑卒中的病理学与脱髓鞘疾病（MS）或其他神经退行性疾病（阿尔茨海默病）至关重要。记住，神经元坏死（嗜酸性细胞质、核缩聚）是梗塞核心中发生坏液化的基本细胞事件。)

<a id='question-143'></a>

### Question 143: Central Nervous System Pathology: Focus 03-43 (the cerebral arteries; the thrombotic infarction i)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Thrombotic stroke pathophysiology, specifically the role of atherosclerosis in large cerebral arteries leading to infarction, and the characteristic histological findings in the brain tissue. (血栓性卒中病理生理学，特别是大脑动脉粥样硬化导致梗死，以及脑组织中的特征性组织学发现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of poorly controlled hypertension, hyperlipidemia, and type 2 diabetes mellitus presents to the emergency department with acute onset of right-sided hemiparesis and aphasia. His symptoms began 3 hours prior to arrival. Vital signs show a blood pressure of 180/105 mmHg. Neurological examination reveals dense right hemiplegia (strength 0/5 in the right upper and lower extremities), right facial droop, and non-fluent aphasia. A non-contrast head CT scan performed immediately shows no evidence of hemorrhage. An MRI of the brain performed 6 hours after symptom onset reveals a well-defined area of restricted diffusion in the left middle cerebral artery (MCA) territory. The patient is treated with intravenous alteplase (tPA) within the therapeutic window. Three days later, the patient undergoes a repeat MRI. The follow-up MRI shows a well-demarcated area of infarction in the left MCA territory, characterized by loss of normal gray-white matter differentiation and sulcal effacement. Histopathological examination of a brain biopsy taken from the infarcted area reveals a pale, swollen tissue with loss of cellular detail and nuclear pyknosis. Microscopic examination shows liquefactive necrosis with infiltration by neutrophils and macrophages.

Which of the following histological findings is most characteristic of the brain tissue in the infarcted area 3 days after the onset of symptoms?

(A) Presence of Rosenthal fibers within astrocytes.
(B) Formation of a glial scar composed of astrocytes.
(C) Liquefactive necrosis with infiltration by neutrophils and macrophages.
(D) Presence of eosinophilic granular cytoplasm within neurons (eosinophilic neuronal dust).
(E) Formation of a well-defined, firm, yellow-tan infarct core with a surrounding rim of hyperemia.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male with significant vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia, diabetes). This predisposes him to atherosclerosis and thromboembolic events.
*   **Presentation:** Acute onset right hemiparesis and aphasia, classic signs of a left MCA stroke. The time course (3 hours) is critical for potential thrombolysis.
*   **Initial Imaging (CT):** Non-contrast CT is negative for hemorrhage, ruling out hemorrhagic stroke and making ischemic stroke likely. Early CT scans are often normal in ischemic stroke.
*   **MRI (6 hours):** Shows restricted diffusion in the left MCA territory, confirming acute ischemic stroke. Restricted diffusion indicates cytotoxic edema due to failure of ion pumps in ischemic cells.
*   **Treatment:** tPA administration within the window. This aims to lyse the thrombus causing the occlusion.
*   **Follow-up MRI (3 days):** Shows established infarction (loss of gray-white differentiation, sulcal effacement) in the left MCA territory. This indicates irreversible tissue damage.
*   **Question:** Asks for the characteristic histological finding 3 days after the stroke onset. This timeframe is crucial for understanding the evolution of ischemic injury.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Ischemic Stroke Pathophysiology:** Thrombotic stroke, often due to atherosclerosis in large arteries like the MCA, leads to occlusion and cessation of blood flow. This causes oxygen and glucose deprivation, leading to ATP depletion, failure of ion pumps (Na+/K+ ATPase, Ca2+ ATPase), influx of Na+ and Ca2+, cellular swelling (cytotoxic edema), and ultimately cell death.
*   **Timeline of Ischemic Injury:**
    *   **0-6 hours:** Reversible injury. Neurons are still viable but metabolically compromised. Restricted diffusion on DWI MRI.
    *   **6-24 hours:** Irreversible injury begins. Neuronal death starts.
    *   **24-72 hours:** Peak of infarction. Liquefactive necrosis develops. Neutrophils infiltrate (peak around 24-48 hours), followed by macrophages (peak around 72 hours). The tissue becomes soft and pale.
    *   **>72 hours:** Macrophages clear debris. Astrocytes proliferate at the periphery, forming a glial scar. Cavitation may occur.
*   **Liquefactive Necrosis:** This is the characteristic type of necrosis seen in the brain following ischemia. It results from the enzymatic digestion of dead cells, primarily by hydrolytic enzymes released from lysosomes of dead cells and inflammatory cells (neutrophils, macrophages). This process leads to the formation of a liquid, viscous mass.
*   **Inflammatory Response:** Neutrophils are the first inflammatory cells to arrive (around 24 hours), followed by macrophages (around 48-72 hours), which phagocytose cellular debris.
*   **Histological Features at 3 Days:** The brain tissue will show liquefactive necrosis, characterized by loss of cellular architecture, nuclear pyknosis (shrinkage and condensation of the nucleus), karyorrhexis (fragmentation of the nucleus), and karyolysis (dissolution of the nucleus). There will be significant infiltration by neutrophils and macrophages.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Presence of Rosenthal fibers within astrocytes:** Rosenthal fibers are thick, elongated, eosinophilic structures found within astrocytes. They are characteristic of chronic injury, particularly in conditions like Alexander disease or chronic gliosis, not the acute phase (3 days) of ischemic infarction.
*   **(B) Formation of a glial scar composed of astrocytes:** Glial scarring (gliosis) is a response to brain injury, where astrocytes proliferate and form a fibrous scar. This process begins around 1-2 weeks after the infarct and becomes prominent later, not typically the dominant feature at 3 days.
*   **(D) Presence of eosinophilic granular cytoplasm within neurons (eosinophilic neuronal dust):** Eosinophilic neuronal dust represents the final stage of neuronal death, where the cell breaks down completely, leaving behind granular eosinophilic debris. While neuronal death occurs in infarction, this specific finding is more characteristic of the later stages (weeks to months) of infarction resolution, not the peak necrotic phase at 3 days.
*   **(E) Formation of a well-defined, firm, yellow-tan infarct core with a surrounding rim of hyperemia:** This description is more characteristic of a *hemorrhagic* infarct, where blood vessels rupture within the necrotic tissue, leading to hemorrhage. While hemorrhagic transformation can occur in ischemic infarcts, the primary histological feature at 3 days in a *thrombotic* infarct (especially after tPA) is liquefactive necrosis, not a firm, yellow-tan core with hyperemia (which describes the early stages before liquefaction or hemorrhagic transformation). The infarct at 3 days is typically pale and soft due to liquefaction.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                     | Thrombotic Infarct (3 days)                                  | Hemorrhagic Infarct                                     | Chronic Infarct (Weeks/Months)                          |
| :-------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------ |
| **Necrosis Type**           | Liquefactive                                                 | Coagulative (initially), then liquefactive/hemorrhagic | Cavitation, Gliosis                                     |
| **Cellular Detail**         | Loss of detail, nuclear pyknosis/karyorrhexis/karyolysis     | Loss of detail, hemorrhage                               | Loss of detail, debris                                 |
| **Inflammatory Cells**      | Neutrophils (peak 24-48h), Macrophages (peak 72h)           | Neutrophils, Macrophages, Erythrocytes                  | Macrophages, Astrocytes                                 |
| **Tissue Consistency**      | Soft, pale, swollen                                           | Firm, yellow-tan, hemorrhagic                             | Cystic cavity, glial scar                               |
| **Key Histological Features** | Liquefaction, inflammatory infiltrate                       | Hemorrhage, hemosiderin-laden macrophages                | Glial scar, Rosenthal fibers (sometimes), neuronal dust |
| **Associated Conditions**   | Atherosclerosis, embolism, vasculitis, hypertension, diabetes | Hypertensive vasculopathy, amyloid angiopathy, AVMs     | Chronic neurological deficits                           |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The hallmark histological feature of an ischemic infarct in the brain 24-72 hours after onset is liquefactive necrosis with an influx of inflammatory cells (neutrophils and macrophages). This contrasts with coagulative necrosis seen in most other tissues and the later stages of gliosis or cavitation. Differentiating the timing of histological changes is crucial for understanding stroke pathology. Key differentials include hemorrhagic transformation (which adds hemorrhage and hemosiderin) and chronic infarcts (which show gliosis and cavitation).

<a id='question-144'></a>

### Question 144: Central Nervous System Pathology: Focus 03-44 (anemic (white) infarct.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating between ischemic (red) and embolic (white/anemic) cerebral infarcts based on pathological features and clinical context, understanding the underlying mechanisms of tissue damage in each type. (区分脑梗死类型：红梗死 vs 白梗死，理解病理机制)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of atrial fibrillation (on warfarin, INR 2.5), hypertension, and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute onset left-sided hemiparesis and aphasia. NIH Stroke Scale score is 18. Non-contrast CT head performed 3 hours post-symptom onset shows no evidence of hemorrhage. CT angiography reveals a complete occlusion of the right middle cerebral artery (MCA). The patient undergoes emergent mechanical thrombectomy, successfully restoring blood flow. Three days later, the patient develops signs of increased intracranial pressure (ICP), including headache, nausea, vomiting, and altered mental status. Repeat CT head reveals a large, well-defined area of hypodensity in the right MCA territory. Neuropathological examination of brain tissue obtained during a subsequent craniotomy for ICP management shows a pale, firm area with loss of gray-white matter differentiation and liquefactive necrosis. Microscopic examination reveals coagulative necrosis with preserved cellular outlines, minimal inflammation, and absence of hemorrhage.

Which of the following pathological findings is MOST characteristic of the infarct described in this patient?

(A) Marked hemorrhage within the infarcted tissue, with hemosiderin-laden macrophages.
(B) Presence of a central zone of liquefactive necrosis surrounded by a rim of gliosis.
(C) A pale, firm area with preserved cellular outlines and minimal inflammation, consistent with coagulative necrosis.
(D) A hemorrhagic transformation with extravasation of red blood cells into the surrounding parenchyma.
(E) A wedge-shaped area of infarction with a pale center and a hemorrhagic border.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male with risk factors for stroke (atrial fibrillation, hypertension, hyperlipidemia).
- **Presentation:** Acute onset left-sided hemiparesis and aphasia, consistent with a right MCA territory stroke (NIHSS 18 indicates severe stroke).
- **Initial Imaging:** Non-contrast CT head negative for hemorrhage, ruling out hemorrhagic stroke initially. CT angiography confirms right MCA occlusion.
- **Intervention:** Successful mechanical thrombectomy restores blood flow.
- **Complication:** Development of increased ICP 3 days later, suggesting infarct expansion or complication.
- **Repeat Imaging:** CT head shows a large hypodensity in the right MCA territory.
- **Neuropathology:** Brain tissue shows a pale, firm area with loss of gray-white differentiation and liquefactive necrosis. Microscopic examination reveals coagulative necrosis with preserved cellular outlines, minimal inflammation, and absence of hemorrhage.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST characteristic pathological finding.

The key is the clinical context (embolic stroke due to atrial fibrillation, successful thrombectomy) and the neuropathological description (pale, firm, coagulative necrosis, minimal inflammation, *absence of hemorrhage*). This points towards an embolic (white/anemic) infarct.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Mechanism of Ischemic Stroke:** Ischemic stroke occurs due to interruption of blood supply to the brain. This can be caused by thrombosis (in situ clot formation, often on atherosclerotic plaque) or embolism (clot or debris traveling from elsewhere, e.g., heart in atrial fibrillation).
- **Types of Cerebral Infarcts:**
    - **Embolic (White/Anemic) Infarcts:** Typically caused by emboli (e.g., from the heart). The embolus lodges in a cerebral artery, occluding it. Because the occlusion is often sudden and complete, the tissue distal to the blockage dies rapidly due to severe ischemia. There is minimal time for collateral circulation to develop or for inflammatory cells to infiltrate. The tissue becomes pale, firm, and dry due to coagulative necrosis (protein denaturation, preserved cell outlines). There is typically little or no hemorrhage.
    - **Thrombotic (Red) Infarcts:** Typically occur in the setting of pre-existing atherosclerosis. The occlusion develops more gradually, allowing time for collateral circulation to develop. This leads to less severe ischemia in the core but more congestion and hemorrhage at the periphery of the infarct due to leakage from damaged vessels and impaired venous drainage. The infarct appears redder, softer, and often wedge-shaped (following the arterial distribution). Microscopically, there is more inflammation and evidence of hemorrhage.
- **Pathology in this Case:** The patient had atrial fibrillation, a common source of emboli. The CT angiography confirmed MCA occlusion, consistent with an embolic event. The successful thrombectomy restored flow, but the initial period of severe ischemia likely caused an embolic infarct. The neuropathological description (pale, firm, coagulative necrosis, minimal inflammation, *no hemorrhage*) perfectly matches the characteristics of an embolic (white/anemic) infarct. The liquefactive necrosis described later is a secondary phenomenon occurring days after the initial infarct, representing tissue breakdown.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Marked hemorrhage within the infarcted tissue, with hemosiderin-laden macrophages. This describes a hemorrhagic transformation, which can occur in *either* red or white infarcts, but is more common and pronounced in red infarcts due to the initial congestion and vascular damage. The vignette specifically states "absence of hemorrhage" microscopically at the time of the described pathology. Hemosiderin-laden macrophages indicate older hemorrhage. This is incorrect.
- (B): Presence of a central zone of liquefactive necrosis surrounded by a rim of gliosis. Liquefactive necrosis is characteristic of bacterial or fungal infections or abscesses in the brain, or later stages of ischemic infarcts (days to weeks post-infarct) where enzymatic digestion occurs. While liquefaction can occur later in an infarct, the primary description in the vignette (pale, firm, coagulative necrosis) points to an earlier stage of an embolic infarct. Gliosis is a chronic response. This is incorrect as the primary description.
- (D): A hemorrhagic transformation with extravasation of red blood cells into the surrounding parenchyma. Similar to (A), this describes hemorrhagic transformation, which is more typical of red infarcts and contradicts the vignette's description of "absence of hemorrhage." This is incorrect.
- (E): A wedge-shaped area of infarction with a pale center and a hemorrhagic border. This is the classic description of a thrombotic (red) infarct, where gradual occlusion allows for collateral flow but also leads to congestion and hemorrhage at the periphery. The vignette describes a pale, firm area without hemorrhage, characteristic of an embolic (white) infarct. This is incorrect.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Embolic (White/Anemic) Infarct | Thrombotic (Red) Infarct |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|---------------------------|
| **Cause**            | Embolus (e.g., heart)           | Thrombosis (in situ)      |
| **Onset**            | Sudden                          | Gradual                   |
| **Occlusion**        | Complete                        | Often incomplete          |
| **Collateral Flow**  | Minimal                         | More developed            |
| **Appearance**       | Pale, firm, dry                 | Red, soft, often wedge-shaped |
| **Hemorrhage**       | Minimal/Absent                  | Often present at border   |
| **Necrosis Type**    | Coagulative                     | Coagulative (initially) -> Liquefactive |
| **Inflammation**     | Minimal (early)                 | More prominent            |
| **Example**          | Atrial fibrillation stroke      | Atherosclerosis stroke    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key difference between embolic (white) and thrombotic (red) cerebral infarcts lies in the speed of occlusion and the development of collateral circulation. Embolic infarcts are typically pale, firm, and lack hemorrhage due to rapid, complete occlusion. Thrombotic infarcts are often redder, softer, and may show hemorrhage due to slower occlusion allowing for collateral flow and congestion. Remember the clinical context (atrial fibrillation suggests embolism) and the specific pathological description (pale, firm, no hemorrhage) to differentiate. Differential diagnoses include hemorrhagic stroke, brain tumor, abscess.

<a id='question-145'></a>

### Question 145: Central Nervous System Pathology: Focus 03-45 (Can be due to embolic occlusion, most often due to )
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathophysiology, clinical presentation, and diagnostic findings of cerebral infarction (stroke), specifically differentiating between ischemic stroke due to embolic occlusion (often cardioembolic) and other causes, focusing on the characteristic pathological changes and clinical context. (本题考察脑梗死（中风）的病理生理学、临床表现和诊断发现，特别是区分由栓塞性闭塞（通常是心源性栓塞）引起的缺血性卒中与其他原因，侧重于其特征性病理变化和临床背景。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of atrial fibrillation (on warfarin, INR 2.5), hypertension, and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with sudden onset of right-sided hemiparesis, facial droop, and aphasia. Symptoms began 3 hours prior to arrival. Neurological examination reveals a NIH Stroke Scale (NIHSS) score of 18. Non-contrast head CT performed immediately shows no evidence of hemorrhage. An emergent CT angiogram (CTA) reveals occlusion of the left middle cerebral artery (MCA) M1 segment. The patient is deemed a candidate for mechanical thrombectomy. Following successful recanalization, a follow-up MRI performed 72 hours later shows a well-defined area of infarction in the left MCA territory, characterized by cytotoxic edema, loss of gray-white matter differentiation, and restricted diffusion on DWI sequences. Histopathological examination of the infarcted tissue obtained during a subsequent craniotomy for managing cerebral edema reveals a pale, softened brain parenchyma with loss of neuronal nuclei and glial cell swelling within the affected area. Immunohistochemical staining for neuronal markers (NeuN) shows a marked reduction in positive cells within the infarct core compared to the surrounding penumbra.

Which of the following pathological findings is MOST characteristic of the irreversible neuronal damage observed in this patient's cerebral infarction?

(A) Liquefactive necrosis with neutrophilic infiltration and abscess formation.
(B) Coagulative necrosis with eosinophilic cytoplasm, pyknotic nuclei, and preservation of tissue architecture.
(C) Necrosis characterized by loss of neuronal nuclei, glial cell swelling, and breakdown of the blood-brain barrier leading to vasogenic edema.
(D) Demyelination with oligodendrocyte loss and microglial proliferation, sparing neuronal cell bodies.
(E) Ischemic cell death mediated by caspase activation and mitochondrial dysfunction, resulting in apoptotic bodies.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male with risk factors for stroke (atrial fibrillation, hypertension, hyperlipidemia). Atrial fibrillation is a major risk factor for cardioembolic stroke.
- **Presentation:** Sudden onset neurological deficits (right hemiparesis, facial droop, aphasia) consistent with a left MCA stroke. NIHSS score of 18 indicates a severe stroke.
- **Timeline:** Symptoms started 3 hours ago, placing the patient within the window for acute stroke interventions.
- **Initial Imaging:** Non-contrast CT rules out hemorrhage.
- **Vascular Imaging:** CTA confirms occlusion of the left MCA M1 segment, strongly suggesting an ischemic stroke due to embolic occlusion (likely from atrial fibrillation).
- **Intervention:** Mechanical thrombectomy successfully recanalizes the artery.
- **Follow-up Imaging (MRI):** Shows infarction in the left MCA territory with characteristic features of acute ischemic stroke (cytotoxic edema, loss of gray-white differentiation, DWI restriction).
- **Histopathology:** Pale, softened brain parenchyma, loss of neuronal nuclei, glial cell swelling.
- **Immunohistochemistry:** Reduced NeuN staining in the infarct core.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST characteristic pathological finding of *irreversible* neuronal damage in this *cerebral infarction*.

The key clues point towards an ischemic stroke (likely embolic from atrial fibrillation) leading to irreversible neuronal damage. The question asks for the specific pathological features of this damage.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Ischemic Stroke Pathophysiology:** Occlusion of a cerebral artery leads to a rapid decrease in blood flow and oxygen supply (ischemia) to the downstream brain tissue.
- **Cellular Response to Ischemia:** Neurons are highly sensitive to oxygen deprivation. Initially, there is a failure of ion pumps (Na+/K+ ATPase), leading to depolarization, influx of calcium, and release of excitatory neurotransmitters (glutamate). This causes excitotoxicity.
- **Irreversible Damage:** If ischemia persists beyond a critical threshold (typically 4-6 hours for neurons), irreversible damage occurs. This involves:
    - **Neuronal Death:** Loss of neuronal nuclei (pyknosis, karyorrhexis), shrinkage of cytoplasm, and eventual breakdown of the cell membrane.
    - **Glial Cell Swelling:** Astrocytes and microglia swell (cytotoxic edema).
    - **Breakdown of Blood-Brain Barrier (BBB):** Increased permeability of the BBB allows plasma proteins and fluid to leak into the brain parenchyma, contributing to vasogenic edema.
    - **Inflammation:** Infiltration of inflammatory cells (neutrophils, macrophages) occurs later (after 24-48 hours), contributing to liquefactive necrosis.
- **Histopathology of Infarction:** In the early stages (hours to days), the infarcted tissue appears pale and softened. Microscopically, neuronal loss, glial swelling, and vascular changes are seen. Later, liquefactive necrosis develops, characterized by enzymatic digestion of the tissue, leading to the formation of a cystic cavity.
- **NeuN Staining:** NeuN is a marker for mature neurons. Its reduction in the infarct core confirms neuronal loss.
- **Option Analysis:**
    - (A) Liquefactive necrosis with neutrophilic infiltration and abscess formation: This occurs later in the evolution of the infarct (days to weeks) and is associated with inflammation and bacterial infection (abscess). It's not the *initial* characteristic feature of irreversible neuronal damage.
    - (B) Coagulative necrosis with eosinophilic cytoplasm, pyknotic nuclei, and preservation of tissue architecture: This is characteristic of ischemic injury in most solid organs (e.g., heart, kidney) but *not* the brain. The brain undergoes liquefactive necrosis due to its high lipid content and lack of fibrous stroma.
    - (C) Necrosis characterized by loss of neuronal nuclei, glial cell swelling, and breakdown of the blood-brain barrier leading to vasogenic edema: This accurately describes the early pathological changes associated with irreversible neuronal damage in cerebral infarction, including the cellular hallmarks (neuronal loss, glial swelling) and the consequence of BBB breakdown (vasogenic edema).
    - (D) Demyelination with oligodendrocyte loss and microglial proliferation, sparing neuronal cell bodies: This describes the pathology of demyelinating diseases like multiple sclerosis, not ischemic stroke. While myelin can be affected in stroke, the primary target is neurons.
    - (E) Ischemic cell death mediated by caspase activation and mitochondrial dysfunction, resulting in apoptotic bodies: While apoptosis (programmed cell death) can occur in ischemia, the predominant form of cell death in the core of a large cerebral infarct is necrosis, characterized by cell swelling, membrane rupture, and inflammation, rather than the formation of apoptotic bodies. Apoptosis might play a role in the penumbra or in specific cell types, but necrosis is the hallmark of the irreversible core damage.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Liquefactive necrosis occurs, but it's a later stage, and abscess formation implies infection, which is not described. The *initial* irreversible damage involves neuronal loss and glial swelling before extensive liquefaction. Test-takers might confuse the later stages of infarction with this option.
- (B): Coagulative necrosis is incorrect for the brain. The brain undergoes liquefactive necrosis. Test-takers might incorrectly apply the definition of necrosis seen in other organs to the brain.
- (D): Demyelination is characteristic of diseases like multiple sclerosis, not the primary pathology of ischemic stroke, which targets neurons first. Test-takers might confuse CNS pathology in general.
- (E): While apoptosis is a form of cell death, the core of a large cerebral infarct typically undergoes necrosis, not apoptosis. Apoptosis involves cell shrinkage and formation of apoptotic bodies, contrasting with the swelling and rupture seen in necrosis. Test-takers might overemphasize the role of apoptosis in ischemia without distinguishing the predominant mechanism in the infarct core.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Cerebral Infarction (Ischemic Stroke) | Coagulative Necrosis (e.g., Myocardial Infarct) | Liquefactive Necrosis (e.g., Brain Abscess) | Demyelination (e.g., Multiple Sclerosis) | Apoptosis (Programmed Cell Death) |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------ | :--------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------- |
| **Primary Cell Type**   | Neurons, Glia                         | Parenchymal Cells (e.g., Myocytes)             | Neurons, Glia                                | Oligodendrocytes, Myelin Sheath         | Various Cell Types                |
| **Necrosis Type**       | Liquefactive                           | Coagulative                                    | Liquefactive                                 | N/A (Primary target is myelin)          | N/A (Programmed)                  |
| **Key Features**        | Neuronal loss, glial swelling, BBB breakdown, cytotoxic/vasogenic edema | Eosinophilic cytoplasm, pyknotic nuclei, preserved architecture | Enzymatic digestion, cystic cavity formation, neutrophilic infiltrate | Oligodendrocyte loss, microglial proliferation, demyelinated axons | Cell shrinkage, blebbing, apoptotic bodies, caspase activation |
| **Typical Cause**       | Vascular Occlusion (Embolus/Thrombus)  | Ischemia (e.g., MI), Hypoxia                   | Infection, Ischemia (later stage)             | Autoimmune attack                       | Various stimuli (e.g., DNA damage) |
| **BBB Involvement**     | Yes (Breakdown)                        | No                                             | Yes (Often associated with inflammation)      | Yes (Inflammation)                      | Variable                          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Cerebral ischemia leads to liquefactive necrosis, characterized by neuronal loss, glial swelling, and breakdown of the blood-brain barrier. This differs from coagulative necrosis seen in other organs and demyelination seen in diseases like MS. While apoptosis occurs, necrosis is the predominant mechanism of irreversible neuronal death in the infarct core. Key differentials include other causes of stroke (hemorrhagic), other CNS pathologies (MS, tumors), and different types of cell death (apoptosis vs. necrosis).

<a id='question-146'></a>

### Question 146: Central Nervous System Pathology: Focus 03-46 (from cardiac chambersand less frequently due to at)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathological features and molecular mechanisms underlying **Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease (CJD)**, specifically focusing on the characteristic spongiform changes, prion protein (PrPSc) accumulation, and its relation to neuronal dysfunction and death. (克雅氏-布氏病 (CJD) 的病理特征和分子机制，特别是海绵状变性、朊病毒蛋白 (PrPSc) 的积累及其与神经元功能障碍和死亡的关系。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male presents to the emergency department with rapidly progressive dementia, ataxia, and myoclonus over the past 6 months. His cognitive decline has been severe, affecting memory, executive function, and language. Neurological examination reveals pronounced cerebellar signs including dysmetria and intention tremor, along with generalized myoclonic jerks. MRI of the brain shows diffuse cortical and basal ganglia hyperintensities on DWI (diffusion-weighted imaging) and FLAIR (fluid-attenuated inversion recovery) sequences, with normal findings on T1-weighted imaging. CSF analysis reveals a normal cell count and glucose level, but elevated protein levels (75 mg/dL, normal <45 mg/dL) and the presence of 14-3-3 protein. EEG shows periodic sharp wave complexes. A brain biopsy is performed. Histopathological examination reveals neuronal loss, astrogliosis, and prominent spongiform changes (vacuolation) within the neuropil, particularly in the cerebral cortex and cerebellum. Immunohistochemical staining shows diffuse deposition of abnormal prion protein (PrPSc) in a granular pattern throughout the neuropil, often associated with amyloid plaques (kuru plaques). Western blot analysis of the brain tissue confirms the presence of PrPSc.

What is the most likely underlying pathological mechanism responsible for the observed spongiform changes and neuronal loss in this patient?

(A) Formation of intracellular inclusions composed of hyperphosphorylated tau protein, leading to neurofibrillary tangles and neuronal dysfunction.
(B) Accumulation of misfolded alpha-synuclein protein aggregates (Lewy bodies) within neuronal cell bodies and processes, disrupting synaptic function and causing neuronal death.
(C) Conversion of the normal cellular prion protein (PrPC) into a misfolded, protease-resistant isoform (PrPSc), which aggregates and induces further misfolding, leading to neuronal damage and spongiform degeneration.
(D) Deposition of amyloid-beta peptide aggregates (senile plaques) extracellularly and intracellular neurofibrillary tangles composed of hyperphosphorylated tau protein, causing synaptic dysfunction and neuroinflammation.
(E) Accumulation of TDP-43 protein aggregates within neuronal cell bodies and processes, leading to cytoplasmic inclusions and nuclear abnormalities, resulting in frontotemporal lobar degeneration.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** Rapidly progressive dementia, ataxia, myoclonus in an elderly patient. This clinical triad is highly suggestive of Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease (CJD).
*   **MRI Findings:** Diffuse cortical and basal ganglia hyperintensities on DWI/FLAIR are characteristic imaging findings in sporadic CJD (sCJD).
*   **CSF Analysis:** Elevated protein and presence of 14-3-3 protein are supportive markers for CJD, although not entirely specific.
*   **EEG Findings:** Periodic sharp wave complexes are a classic, though not universal, finding in sCJD.
*   **Brain Biopsy (Histopathology):** Neuronal loss, astrogliosis, and prominent spongiform changes (vacuolation) are the hallmark neuropathological features of CJD.
*   **Immunohistochemistry:** Diffuse granular deposition of abnormal prion protein (PrPSc) confirms the diagnosis of prion disease.
*   **Western Blot:** Confirms the presence of the protease-resistant PrPSc isoform.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying pathological mechanism responsible for the spongiform changes and neuronal loss.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **CJD Pathogenesis:** CJD is a fatal neurodegenerative disease caused by prions. Prions are infectious agents composed primarily of the misfolded prion protein (PrPSc).
*   **PrPC vs. PrPSc:** The normal cellular prion protein (PrPC) is a ubiquitous glycoprotein found on the cell surface, particularly abundant in neurons. PrPSc is an abnormal isoform of PrPC that has undergone a conformational change.
*   **Mechanism of Misfolding:** PrPSc can induce the conformational conversion of normal PrPC molecules into more PrPSc. This is a self-propagating process, leading to an exponential accumulation of PrPSc.
*   **Aggregation and Plaque Formation:** PrPSc molecules aggregate, forming oligomers and fibrils. These aggregates can deposit extracellularly as amyloid plaques (kuru plaques) or intracellularly.
*   **Spongiform Degeneration:** The accumulation of PrPSc and its aggregates, along with neuronal loss and reactive astrogliosis, leads to the characteristic spongiform appearance (vacuolation) in the neuropil, resembling a sponge under the microscope. This is thought to result from neuronal injury and death triggered by PrPSc accumulation, possibly through disruption of cellular processes, induction of apoptosis, or activation of inflammatory pathways.
*   **Neuronal Loss:** The accumulation of PrPSc and the resulting cellular dysfunction ultimately lead to widespread neuronal death, particularly in the cortex, cerebellum, basal ganglia, and thalamus.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Formation of intracellular inclusions composed of hyperphosphorylated tau protein, leading to neurofibrillary tangles and neuronal dysfunction.** This describes the pathology of Alzheimer's disease and other tauopathies (e.g., progressive supranuclear palsy, corticobasal degeneration). While Alzheimer's causes dementia, the characteristic pathology is neurofibrillary tangles (tau) and amyloid plaques (Aβ), not spongiform changes or PrPSc deposition. The clinical presentation (rapid progression, myoclonus, ataxia) is also less typical for Alzheimer's.
*   **(B) Accumulation of misfolded alpha-synuclein protein aggregates (Lewy bodies) within neuronal cell bodies and processes, disrupting synaptic function and causing neuronal death.** This describes the pathology of Parkinson's disease and Lewy body dementia. Lewy bodies are intracytoplasmic inclusions. While Lewy body dementia can cause dementia and sometimes parkinsonism/ataxia, the hallmark pathology is not spongiform changes or PrPSc deposition.
*   **(D) Deposition of amyloid-beta peptide aggregates (senile plaques) extracellularly and intracellular neurofibrillary tangles composed of hyperphosphorylated tau protein, causing synaptic dysfunction and neuroinflammation.** This is the classic neuropathology of Alzheimer's disease. As in option A, this does not explain the spongiform changes or PrPSc deposition seen in CJD.
*   **(E) Accumulation of TDP-43 protein aggregates within neuronal cell bodies and processes, leading to cytoplasmic inclusions and nuclear abnormalities, resulting in frontotemporal lobar degeneration.** This describes the pathology of frontotemporal lobar degeneration (FTLD) and amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS). The clinical presentation (behavioral changes, language deficits, motor neuron signs) and pathology (TDP-43 inclusions) are distinct from CJD.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Creutzfeldt-Jakob Disease (CJD) | Alzheimer's Disease (AD) | Parkinson's Disease (PD) / Lewy Body Dementia (LBD) | Frontotemporal Lobar Degeneration (FTLD) |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------ | :----------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ | :---------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Pathology**   | PrPSc accumulation, Spongiform changes | Aβ plaques, Tau tangles | Lewy bodies (α-synuclein)                         | TDP-43 inclusions                         |
| **Key Protein**         | PrPSc                           | Aβ, Tau                  | α-synuclein                                       | TDP-43                                    |
| **Microscopic Feature** | Spongiform degeneration         | Plaques, Tangles         | Lewy bodies                                       | Inclusions                                |
| **Clinical Presentation** | Rapid dementia, ataxia, myoclonus | Gradual dementia, memory loss | Parkinsonism, dementia, fluctuations, hallucinations | Behavioral/language changes, motor neuron signs |
| **MRI**                 | DWI/FLAIR hyperintensities (cortical ribboning) | Atrophy                  | Variable                                         | Variable, often frontal/temporal atrophy |
| **CSF**                 | 14-3-3 protein, elevated protein | Aβ42 low, Tau high       | Normal or α-synuclein low                         | Normal or TDP-43 high                     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
CJD is characterized by the conversion of normal prion protein (PrPC) to a misfolded, protease-resistant form (PrPSc), leading to aggregation, neuronal damage, and characteristic spongiform changes. This distinguishes it from other neurodegenerative diseases like Alzheimer's (Aβ plaques, tau tangles), Parkinson's/LBD (Lewy bodies), and FTLD (TDP-43 inclusions). Rapidly progressive dementia with myoclonus and ataxia, along with specific MRI and CSF findings, strongly suggests CJD.

<a id='question-147'></a>

### Question 147: Central Nervous System Pathology
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating between thrombotic and embolic stroke based on pathological findings, specifically the presence or absence of hemorrhage within the infarct (Hemorrhagic Infarction vs. Anemic Infarction) (脑梗死类型鉴别：出血性梗死 vs. 贫血性梗死).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of atrial fibrillation (on warfarin, INR 2.5) and hypertension presents to the emergency department with sudden onset left-sided weakness and slurred speech. Neurological examination reveals right hemiparesis (strength 2/5) and aphasia. Non-contrast head CT performed 3 hours post-symptom onset shows a wedge-shaped area of hypodensity in the left middle cerebral artery (MCA) territory. The CT scan also reveals a distinct area of hyperdensity within the hypodense region. Lumbar puncture is performed 24 hours later and reveals xanthochromia and elevated red blood cell count. MRI performed 48 hours later shows a well-defined area of restricted diffusion in the left MCA territory with T2*-weighted hypointensity consistent with hemosiderin deposition.

Which of the following pathological findings is MOST likely present in the affected brain tissue?

(A) Liquefactive necrosis with neutrophilic infiltrate and hemosiderin-laden macrophages surrounding the infarct core.
(B) Coagulative necrosis with eosinophilic cytoplasm, loss of nuclei, and preservation of tissue architecture within the infarct core.
(C) Necrosis with preservation of cellular outlines, presence of Mallory bodies, and a surrounding zone of gliosis.
(D) Necrosis with liquefaction, formation of a cystic cavity, and a prominent inflammatory response dominated by lymphocytes and plasma cells.
(E) Necrosis with preservation of cellular outlines, presence of eosinophilic glassy homogeneous plaques, and a surrounding zone of gliosis.

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male with atrial fibrillation (a major risk factor for cardioembolic stroke) and hypertension.
*   **Presentation:** Sudden onset focal neurological deficits (left hemiparesis, aphasia) consistent with a stroke affecting the right cerebral hemisphere (specifically, the left MCA territory).
*   **Initial CT (3 hours post-onset):** Shows a wedge-shaped hypodensity (infarct) in the left MCA territory. Crucially, it also shows a *hyperdense* area *within* the hypodensity. This hyperdensity represents hemorrhage within the infarct.
*   **Lumbar Puncture (24 hours post-onset):** Xanthochromia (yellowish discoloration of CSF due to bilirubin from RBC breakdown) and elevated RBC count confirm the presence of blood in the subarachnoid space, likely originating from the intracerebral hemorrhage.
*   **MRI (48 hours post-onset):** Restricted diffusion (acute infarct) and T2*-weighted hypointensity (hemosiderin deposition, indicating prior hemorrhage) further confirm an ischemic stroke complicated by hemorrhage.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely pathological finding in the affected brain tissue.

The combination of atrial fibrillation (source of emboli), sudden onset focal deficits, a wedge-shaped infarct (characteristic of arterial occlusion), and the presence of hemorrhage within the infarct (hyperdensity on CT, xanthochromia on LP, hemosiderin on MRI) strongly points towards an *embolic stroke* that has undergone *hemorrhagic transformation*. Embolic strokes often occlude arteries abruptly, leading to severe ischemia and subsequent breakdown of the blood-brain barrier, allowing blood to enter the infarcted tissue.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Embolic Stroke:** An embolus (often from the heart in atrial fibrillation) travels through the bloodstream and lodges in a cerebral artery, causing ischemia distal to the occlusion.
*   **Hemorrhagic Transformation:** Ischemic injury leads to disruption of the blood-brain barrier and damage to vessel walls. This allows extravasation of red blood cells into the infarcted parenchyma. The risk is higher in large infarcts, strokes involving the basal ganglia or thalamus, and in patients on anticoagulation (like this patient on warfarin).
*   **Pathology of Hemorrhagic Infarction:** The infarct core undergoes necrosis (typically liquefactive necrosis in the brain). The surrounding area shows inflammation (neutrophils initially, then macrophages). Hemosiderin-laden macrophages (siderophages) are characteristic, resulting from the breakdown of extravasated red blood cells. The presence of hemorrhage distinguishes it from a typical anemic (pale) infarct.
*   **Pathology of Thrombotic Stroke:** Often occurs in areas of pre-existing atherosclerosis. The occlusion develops more gradually, allowing for potential collateral circulation. The infarct is often less sharply demarcated than an embolic infarct. Hemorrhagic transformation can occur but is less common than with embolic strokes, especially large ones.
*   **Pathology of Anemic Infarction:** The infarct tissue is pale due to the lack of blood supply. Necrosis occurs, followed by inflammation and eventual gliosis.
*   **Pathology of Coagulative Necrosis:** Typically seen in solid organs (like the heart or kidney) due to ischemia. Tissue architecture is preserved for some time, cells become eosinophilic. This is *not* the typical pattern in the brain.
*   **Pathology of Liquefactive Necrosis:** Characteristic of ischemic injury in the brain. Enzymes released from damaged cells and inflammatory cells digest the tissue, leading to liquefaction and eventual cyst formation.
*   **Pathology of Mallory Bodies:** Eosinophilic cytoplasmic inclusions found in hepatocytes, characteristic of alcoholic liver disease. Irrelevant to brain infarcts.
*   **Pathology of Eosinophilic Glassy Homogeneous Plaques:** Characteristic of fibrinoid necrosis, often seen in vasculitis or malignant hypertension. Not typical for ischemic stroke.

Therefore, the most likely pathological finding is liquefactive necrosis within the infarct core, accompanied by inflammation and the presence of hemosiderin-laden macrophages due to the hemorrhagic transformation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Correct.** Describes the typical pathology of a hemorrhagic infarct: liquefactive necrosis (brain tissue response to ischemia), neutrophilic infiltrate (acute inflammation), and hemosiderin-laden macrophages (siderophages) resulting from the breakdown of extravasated red blood cells. This aligns perfectly with the clinical presentation of an embolic stroke with hemorrhagic transformation.
- (B): Describes coagulative necrosis. This is the typical pattern of necrosis in solid organs (e.g., myocardial infarction, renal infarction) due to ischemia, where cellular architecture is initially preserved. The brain undergoes liquefactive necrosis due to the high lipid content and enzymatic activity. This is incorrect for a brain infarct.
- (C): Describes features associated with Mallory bodies, which are found in alcoholic liver disease, not brain infarcts. This option is completely irrelevant to the clinical scenario.
- (D): Describes liquefactive necrosis and cyst formation, which occurs later in the evolution of an infarct. While liquefaction is correct for the brain, the description emphasizes cyst formation and a lymphocytic/plasma cell infiltrate, which are features of a more chronic or resolving infarct, not the acute hemorrhagic transformation described. Also, it doesn't mention the key feature of hemorrhage (hemosiderin).
- (E): Describes eosinophilic glassy homogeneous plaques, characteristic of fibrinoid necrosis seen in conditions like malignant hypertension or vasculitis affecting blood vessels, not the parenchymal necrosis of an ischemic stroke.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                     | Hemorrhagic Infarct (Embolic Stroke) | Thrombotic Stroke (Anemic Infarct) | Coagulative Necrosis (e.g., MI) | Liquefactive Necrosis (e.g., Brain Abscess) | Fibrinoid Necrosis (e.g., Vasculitis) |
| :-------------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :--------------------------------- | :------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------ |
| **Cause**                   | Embolus occluding artery             | Thrombus forming in artery        | Ischemia (solid organs)          | Ischemia/Infection (brain)                  | Immune complex deposition/Vasculitis |
| **Gross Appearance**        | Wedge-shaped, hemorrhagic            | Often less defined, pale           | Pale, firm                       | Soft, liquefied                             | Homogeneous, eosinophilic             |
| **Microscopic Necrosis Type** | Liquefactive                         | Liquefactive                       | Coagulative                      | Liquefactive                                 | Fibrinoid                              |
| **Hemorrhage**              | Common (Hemorrhagic Transformation)  | Less common                        | Rare                             | Rare                                         | Rare                                  |
| **Inflammation**            | Neutrophils, Macrophages (Siderophages) | Neutrophils, Macrophages           | Neutrophils, Macrophages         | Neutrophils, Macrophages                    | Variable                               |
| **Key Feature**             | Hemosiderin-laden macrophages        | Pale infarct core                  | Preserved architecture initially | Cyst formation                              | Eosinophilic plaques                   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Embolic strokes, especially large ones or those occurring in patients on anticoagulation, are prone to hemorrhagic transformation. Pathologically, this manifests as liquefactive necrosis (typical for brain infarcts) with evidence of hemorrhage, including hemosiderin-laden macrophages. Differentiating between anemic and hemorrhagic infarcts is crucial, as the presence of hemorrhage significantly alters prognosis and management. Remember that the brain undergoes liquefactive necrosis, unlike coagulative necrosis seen in solid organs.
