<a id='question-148'></a>

### Question 148: Central Nervous System Pathology: Focus 03-48 (Lacunar Infarcts)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Chronic hypertension leads to small vessel disease (lipohyalinosis and microatheroma formation) in the brain, predisposing to lacunar infarcts, particularly in deep gray matter structures like the basal ganglia and thalamus. (慢性高血压导致脑部小血管病（脂肪透明变性和微动脉粥样硬化），易患腔隙性梗死，尤其是在基底神经节和丘脑等深部灰质结构中。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a 30-year history of poorly controlled hypertension presents to the emergency department with acute onset of right-sided hemiparesis and sensory loss affecting the right face, arm, and leg. His blood pressure upon arrival is 210/110 mmHg. Neurological examination reveals weakness (MRC grade 3/5) in the right upper and lower extremities, decreased sensation to light touch on the right side of the body, and a right homonymous hemianopia. A non-contrast head CT scan shows a small (1.5 cm), well-defined, hypodense lesion in the left internal capsule. MRI of the brain confirms the CT findings and shows no evidence of hemorrhage or large vessel occlusion. The patient's history includes smoking (40 pack-years) and hyperlipidemia treated with atorvastatin. His fasting glucose is 110 mg/dL, and HbA1c is 6.2%. Which of the following pathological changes is most likely responsible for this patient's neurological deficits?

(A) Fibrinoid necrosis of medium-sized arteries with transmural inflammation, characteristic of polyarteritis nodosa.
(B) Hyaline arteriolosclerosis with luminal narrowing and associated microinfarcts in the deep gray matter structures.
(C) Amyloid deposition within the walls of small arteries and arterioles, leading to vessel wall thickening and luminal stenosis.
(D) Demyelination plaques with perivascular lymphocytic infiltration, typically found in the white matter.
(E) Necrotizing granulomas with multinucleated giant cells surrounding a central area of caseous necrosis, often seen in the basal ganglia.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male with long-standing, poorly controlled hypertension (30 years), smoking history, hyperlipidemia. These are major risk factors for chronic small vessel disease.
- **Presentation:** Acute onset of right hemiparesis, sensory loss (face, arm, leg), and right homonymous hemianopia. This constellation of symptoms points to a lesion affecting the left internal capsule (containing corticospinal and corticothalamic tracts) and potentially the optic radiations (passing near the internal capsule).
- **Imaging:** Non-contrast CT and MRI show a small (1.5 cm), well-defined, hypodense lesion in the left internal capsule, without hemorrhage or large vessel occlusion. This morphology is classic for a lacunar infarct.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the pathological change responsible for the lacunar infarct in the context of chronic hypertension.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Lacunar Infarcts:** These are small (<1.5 cm) infarcts occurring in the deep structures of the brain (basal ganglia, thalamus, pons, internal capsule, cerebellum) supplied by small, penetrating arteries (lenticulostriate arteries, thalamoperforating arteries).
- **Etiology:** The most common cause is chronic hypertension, which damages the walls of these small arteries and arterioles.
- **Pathological Changes:** Chronic hypertension leads to two main types of small vessel disease:
    - **Lipohyalinosis:** Thickening of the vessel wall due to deposition of hyaline material (plasma proteins, lipids) and smooth muscle cell proliferation, leading to luminal narrowing.
    - **Microatheroma formation:** Similar to atherosclerosis in larger vessels, but occurring in arterioles.
- **Mechanism:** These changes cause ischemia and eventual infarction (lacunar infarct) in the territory supplied by the affected vessel. The location of the infarct depends on the specific vessel involved. The internal capsule is a common site.
- **Option B:** Describes hyaline arteriolosclerosis (lipohyalinosis) and associated microinfarcts in deep gray matter, which is the hallmark pathology of lacunar infarcts caused by chronic hypertension. This perfectly matches the clinical presentation and imaging findings.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Fibrinoid necrosis and transmural inflammation are characteristic of vasculitis, such as polyarteritis nodosa (PAN). PAN typically affects medium-sized arteries, not the small penetrating arterioles responsible for lacunar infarcts. While PAN can cause neurological deficits, the pathology described is incorrect for a typical hypertensive lacunar infarct.
- (C): Amyloid deposition in vessel walls (cerebral amyloid angiopathy, CAA) typically affects medium-sized cortical and leptomeningeal arteries, leading to lobar hemorrhages, particularly in the elderly. It is not the primary cause of lacunar infarcts in the deep gray matter structures.
- (D): Demyelination plaques with perivascular lymphocytic infiltration are characteristic of multiple sclerosis (MS), a demyelinating disease affecting the central nervous system, primarily the white matter. While MS can cause various neurological deficits, the pathology and typical location (white matter plaques) are distinct from lacunar infarcts in the internal capsule.
- (E): Necrotizing granulomas with multinucleated giant cells and caseous necrosis are characteristic of tuberculosis (TB). While CNS TB can affect the basal ganglia (tuberculomas), the pathology described is specific to infectious granulomatous inflammation, not the chronic ischemic changes seen in hypertensive small vessel disease.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Lacunar Infarct (Hypertensive) | Polyarteritis Nodosa (PAN) | Cerebral Amyloid Angiopathy (CAA) | Multiple Sclerosis (MS) | CNS Tuberculosis |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------------|-----------------------------------|--------------------------|-------------------|
| **Vessel Size**      | Small arterioles/penetrating arteries | Medium-sized arteries       | Medium-sized cortical/leptomeningeal arteries | White matter myelin | Variable (often meningeal/parenchymal) |
| **Pathology**        | Lipohyalinosis, microatheroma, microinfarct | Fibrinoid necrosis, transmural inflammation | Amyloid deposition        | Demyelination, perivascular inflammation | Granulomas, caseous necrosis |
| **Location**         | Deep gray matter (basal ganglia, thalamus, internal capsule) | Variable, often renal, mesenteric, peripheral nerves | Cerebral cortex, leptomeninges | White matter (periventricular, juxtacortical) | Variable (basal ganglia, meninges) |
| **Clinical Manifestation** | Pure motor/sensory stroke, ataxic hemiparesis, dysarthria-clumsy hand syndrome | Systemic vasculitis symptoms, variable neurological deficits | Lobar hemorrhage, cognitive decline | Variable neurological deficits (optic neuritis, sensory changes, motor weakness, ataxia) | Headache, seizures, focal deficits, meningitis |
| **Risk Factors**     | Chronic hypertension, diabetes, smoking, hyperlipidemia | Hepatitis B/C, HIV, unknown | Age | Young adulthood | Immunocompromise, exposure |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Chronic hypertension is the leading cause of lacunar infarcts, which are small strokes affecting deep brain structures due to damage to small penetrating arteries (lipohyalinosis and microatheroma). Key differentials include other causes of stroke (large vessel occlusion, cardioembolism, hemorrhage), vasculitis (PAN), demyelinating disease (MS), amyloid angiopathy (CAA), and infectious processes (CNS TB). Remember the characteristic location (deep gray matter) and size (<1.5 cm) of lacunar infarcts.

<a id='question-149'></a>

### Question 149: Central Nervous System Pathology: Focus 03-49 (Hypertension, Hyaline Arteriolosclerosis)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Chronic hypertension leads to hyaline arteriolosclerosis in the brain, particularly affecting small penetrating arteries, causing lacunar infarcts and potentially hemorrhagic stroke (e.g., Charcot-Bouchard aneurysms). (慢性高血压导致脑部透明质小动脉硬化，特别是影响小穿通动脉，引起腔隙性梗死和潜在性出血性卒中（如夏科-布夏德动脉瘤）。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old African American male with a 30-year history of poorly controlled hypertension (average BP 180/110 mmHg) presents to the emergency department after experiencing sudden onset of severe headache, nausea, vomiting, and altered mental status. His Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS) score is 10 (E2, V3, M5). Non-contrast head CT scan reveals a deep intraparenchymal hemorrhage in the basal ganglia on the right side. Neurological examination shows left-sided hemiparesis and hemisensory loss. His medical history is significant for hyperlipidemia treated with atorvastatin and type 2 diabetes mellitus treated with metformin. Laboratory studies show elevated blood glucose (250 mg/dL), elevated creatinine (1.8 mg/dL), and normal coagulation profile. A subsequent CT angiogram shows no evidence of large aneurysms or arteriovenous malformations. Brain biopsy performed post-mortem reveals small arteries with thickened walls, narrowed lumens, and eosinophilic, glassy appearance of the vessel walls.

Which of the following pathological findings is most likely responsible for the patient's intraparenchymal hemorrhage?

(A) Amyloid deposition within the walls of small cerebral arteries, leading to vessel fragility.
(B) Fibrinoid necrosis of medium-sized cerebral arteries, associated with immune complex deposition.
(C) Hyaline arteriolosclerosis affecting small penetrating arteries in the basal ganglia.
(D) Atherosclerotic plaque formation in large cerebral arteries, causing embolic events.
(E) Vasculitis involving small to medium-sized cerebral vessels, characterized by inflammatory cell infiltration.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old African American male with long-standing, poorly controlled hypertension. This immediately points towards hypertensive vascular disease.
*   **Presentation:** Sudden severe headache, nausea, vomiting, altered mental status (GCS 10), left hemiparesis, left hemisensory loss. This clinical picture is highly suggestive of an acute intraparenchymal hemorrhage (ICH), likely involving the right basal ganglia given the left-sided deficits.
*   **Imaging:** Non-contrast head CT confirms a deep intraparenchymal hemorrhage in the right basal ganglia. CT angiogram rules out common causes like large aneurysms or AVMs.
*   **Medical History:** Hypertension, hyperlipidemia, type 2 diabetes mellitus. These are risk factors for various vascular pathologies.
*   **Post-mortem Brain Biopsy:** Small arteries with thickened walls, narrowed lumens, and eosinophilic, glassy appearance of the vessel walls. This is the classic description of hyaline arteriolosclerosis.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the pathological finding responsible for the hemorrhage. The combination of chronic hypertension, deep basal ganglia hemorrhage, and the specific biopsy findings strongly implicates hypertensive vasculopathy.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Hypertension & Cerebral Vasculature:** Chronic hypertension exerts high pressure on the walls of small penetrating arteries (lenticulostriate arteries) supplying deep brain structures like the basal ganglia, thalamus, pons, and internal capsule.
*   **Hyaline Arteriolosclerosis:** This is the characteristic vascular change seen in chronic hypertension and diabetes. It involves thickening of the arteriolar walls due to deposition of hyaline material (plasma proteins, lipids, glycosaminoglycans) in the intima and media, leading to luminal narrowing. The vessel walls become rigid and less compliant.
*   **Mechanism of Hemorrhage:** The weakened, rigid walls of these arterioles are prone to rupture, especially during acute hypertensive crises or even spontaneously. Rupture leads to intraparenchymal hemorrhage, often in the characteristic locations supplied by these vessels (basal ganglia, thalamus, pons, cerebellum). Specific types of aneurysms, called Charcot-Bouchard aneurysms, can also form at branching points of these weakened arterioles and rupture.
*   **Biopsy Findings:** The description in the vignette ("thickened walls, narrowed lumens, and eosinophilic, glassy appearance") perfectly matches hyaline arteriolosclerosis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Amyloid deposition within the walls of small cerebral arteries, leading to vessel fragility.** This describes cerebral amyloid angiopathy (CAA). CAA typically affects older individuals and is associated with lobar hemorrhages (cortical/subcortical), not deep hemorrhages like in the basal ganglia. The biopsy finding is also different (amyloid deposits, often Congo red positive). While CAA causes hemorrhage, it's not the primary pathology associated with long-standing hypertension and basal ganglia bleeds.
*   **(B) Fibrinoid necrosis of medium-sized cerebral arteries, associated with immune complex deposition.** This is characteristic of vasculitis (e.g., polyarteritis nodosa) or malignant hypertension. Vasculitis involves inflammation and necrosis of vessel walls. Malignant hypertension can cause fibrinoid necrosis, but the biopsy description ("eosinophilic, glassy appearance") does not fit fibrinoid necrosis (which is characterized by fibrin deposition and inflammatory cells). Also, the clinical presentation is more typical of chronic hypertensive damage than acute vasculitis or malignant hypertension without other systemic signs.
*   **(D) Atherosclerotic plaque formation in large cerebral arteries, causing embolic events.** Atherosclerosis primarily affects large and medium-sized arteries (e.g., carotid, vertebral, middle cerebral artery). While atherosclerosis can lead to ischemic stroke via thromboembolism or stenosis, it is not the direct cause of deep intraparenchymal hemorrhages in the basal ganglia. The biopsy findings also do not describe atherosclerotic plaques (which involve lipid cores, fibrous caps, inflammation).
*   **(E) Vasculitis involving small to medium-sized cerebral vessels, characterized by inflammatory cell infiltration.** As mentioned in (B), vasculitis involves inflammation. The biopsy description lacks any mention of inflammatory cells, which are a hallmark of vasculitis. While vasculitis can cause ICH, the patient's history of chronic hypertension and the specific biopsy findings make hypertensive arteriolosclerosis far more likely.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                     | Hyaline Arteriolosclerosis (C) | Cerebral Amyloid Angiopathy (A) | Fibrinoid Necrosis (B) | Atherosclerosis (D) | Vasculitis (E) |
| :-------------------------- | :----------------------------- | :------------------------------ | :---------------------- | :------------------ | :------------- |
| **Primary Cause**            | Chronic Hypertension, Diabetes  | Age, ApoE4 allele               | Malignant HTN, Vasculitis | Atherogenic Risk Factors | Autoimmune/Infection |
| **Vessel Size Affected**     | Small Arteries/Arterioles      | Small/Medium Arteries           | Medium Arteries         | Large/Medium Arteries | Small/Medium Arteries |
| **Location of Hemorrhage**   | Deep (Basal Ganglia, Thalamus) | Lobar (Cortical/Subcortical)    | Variable                | Ischemic Stroke (Embolism) | Variable       |
| **Biopsy Appearance**        | Thickened wall, narrowed lumen, eosinophilic/glassy hyaline | Amyloid deposits in wall | Fibrin deposition, inflammation, necrosis | Lipid core, fibrous cap | Inflammation, necrosis |
| **Association with HTN**     | Strong                        | Weak                            | Strong (Malignant HTN)  | Indirect            | Possible       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Chronic hypertension is a major cause of deep intraparenchymal hemorrhages, particularly in the basal ganglia, due to hyaline arteriolosclerosis weakening the walls of small penetrating arteries. Differentiating this from other causes of ICH like CAA (lobar bleeds), vasculitis (often systemic symptoms, inflammation on biopsy), or atherosclerosis (ischemic stroke) is crucial. The specific biopsy findings described are pathognomonic for hyaline arteriolosclerosis.

<a id='question-150'></a>

### Question 150: Central Nervous System Pathology
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathophysiology and clinical presentation of cerebral amyloid angiopathy (CAA), specifically differentiating it from other causes of lobar hemorrhage and recognizing the characteristic imaging and pathological findings. (本题考察脑淀粉样血管病 (CAA) 的病理生理学和临床表现，特别是将其与其他小叶出血原因区分开来，并识别其特征性影像学和病理学发现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 72-year-old woman with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department after experiencing sudden onset of severe headache, nausea, and right-sided weakness. Her vital signs show a blood pressure of 180/105 mmHg. A non-contrast head CT scan reveals a left parietal lobe hemorrhage with surrounding edema. The patient has no history of trauma, aneurysm, or arteriovenous malformation (AVM). Neurological examination confirms right hemiparesis and sensory loss. MRI of the brain shows multiple punctate foci of hemosiderin deposition in the cerebral cortex and leptomeninges, particularly prominent in the occipital and parietal lobes, consistent with chronic microhemorrhages. Cerebral angiography is unremarkable for aneurysms or AVMs. A brain biopsy performed several years prior for an unrelated condition showed diffuse amyloid deposition within the walls of small-to-medium sized cortical blood vessels, with Congo red staining positive for amyloid and apple-green birefringence under polarized light. Given this clinical presentation and prior biopsy findings, what is the most likely underlying cause of the patient's lobar hemorrhage?

(A) Hypertensive vasculopathy leading to Charcot-Bouchard microaneurysms
(B) Cerebral amyloid angiopathy (CAA)
(C) Arteriovenous malformation (AVM) rupture
(D) Cerebral aneurysm rupture
(E) Vasculitis associated with systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE)

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 72-year-old woman, elderly patient, increasing risk for degenerative conditions.
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Sudden severe headache, nausea, right-sided weakness – classic signs of intracerebral hemorrhage (ICH).
- **History:** Hypertension and hyperlipidemia – risk factors for both hypertensive vasculopathy and atherosclerosis, but also common in the elderly. No history of trauma, aneurysm, or AVM – makes these less likely primary causes.
- **CT Findings:** Left parietal lobe hemorrhage – lobar hemorrhage.
- **MRI Findings:** Multiple punctate foci of hemosiderin deposition in the cortex and leptomeninges (occipital/parietal lobes) – indicates chronic microhemorrhages, a hallmark of CAA.
- **Angiography:** Unremarkable – rules out large aneurysms or AVMs as the source of the acute hemorrhage.
- **Prior Biopsy:** Diffuse amyloid deposition within small-to-medium sized cortical blood vessels, Congo red positive, apple-green birefringence – definitive pathological evidence of CAA.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the most likely *underlying cause* of the *lobar hemorrhage* given the entire clinical picture, including the prior biopsy.

The combination of lobar hemorrhage in an elderly patient, chronic cortical microhemorrhages on MRI, negative angiography, and prior biopsy confirming amyloid deposition in cerebral vessels strongly points to CAA as the cause.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Cerebral Amyloid Angiopathy (CAA):** A condition characterized by the deposition of amyloid-beta (Aβ) protein, primarily the Aβ42 isoform, within the walls of small-to-medium sized arteries, arterioles, and capillaries in the cerebral cortex and leptomeninges.
- **Pathophysiology:** Aβ deposition weakens the vessel walls, making them prone to rupture, leading to microhemorrhages, lobar hemorrhages, and cognitive decline. The amyloid deposition is often associated with cerebral amyloid plaques found in Alzheimer's disease, but CAA can occur independently or coexist.
- **Clinical Manifestations:** Can be asymptomatic (detected incidentally on imaging), present with cognitive impairment, seizures, or transient neurological deficits. Symptomatic CAA often manifests as recurrent lobar hemorrhages, particularly in the occipital and parietal lobes.
- **Diagnosis:** Suspected based on clinical presentation and imaging (MRI showing cortical microhemorrhages). Definitive diagnosis requires brain biopsy demonstrating amyloid deposition in vessel walls, positive Congo red staining, and apple-green birefringence under polarized light.
- **Differential Diagnosis:** Hypertensive vasculopathy (Charcot-Bouchard aneurysms), AVMs, aneurysms, vasculitis. CAA typically causes lobar hemorrhages, whereas hypertensive vasculopathy often causes hemorrhages in the basal ganglia, thalamus, pons, and cerebellum. CAA is distinguished from AVMs and aneurysms by negative angiography. Vasculitis usually presents with more systemic symptoms and specific imaging findings.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Hypertensive vasculopathy leading to Charcot-Bouchard microaneurysms:** This is a common cause of ICH, especially in elderly patients with hypertension. However, it typically affects deep brain structures (basal ganglia, thalamus, pons, cerebellum) rather than the cortex (lobar hemorrhage). While the patient has hypertension, the MRI findings of *cortical* microhemorrhages and the prior biopsy confirming amyloid deposition make CAA much more likely. Charcot-Bouchard aneurysms are saccular outpouchings of small arteries weakened by chronic hypertension.
- (C): **Arteriovenous malformation (AVM) rupture:** AVMs are congenital vascular abnormalities that can rupture and cause hemorrhage. However, angiography was unremarkable, effectively ruling out a large AVM as the source of this hemorrhage. While small AVMs might be missed, the constellation of findings (age, cortical microhemorrhages, prior biopsy) strongly favors CAA.
- (D): **Cerebral aneurysm rupture:** Aneurysms are focal dilatations of arteries, often at branching points. Rupture typically causes subarachnoid hemorrhage (SAH), although they can sometimes cause ICH if they rupture into the brain parenchyma. Angiography was negative for aneurysms, making this unlikely. Furthermore, aneurysms are less commonly associated with the pattern of chronic cortical microhemorrhages seen on MRI.
- (E): **Vasculitis associated with systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE):** Vasculitis can affect cerebral vessels and cause hemorrhage or infarction. However, SLE vasculitis typically presents with more systemic symptoms (rash, joint pain, fever, renal involvement) and often shows specific imaging features (e.g., multiple infarcts, diffuse white matter changes). The patient's presentation lacks these features, and the prior biopsy specifically identified amyloid deposition, not inflammatory infiltrates characteristic of vasculitis.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Cerebral Amyloid Angiopathy (CAA) | Hypertensive Vasculopathy | AVM Rupture | Cerebral Aneurysm Rupture | Vasculitis (e.g., SLE) |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :------------------------ | :---------- | :------------------------ | :-------------------- |
| **Typical Location**    | Lobar (cortex, leptomeninges)     | Deep structures (basal ganglia, thalamus) | Variable   | SAH (can cause ICH)      | Variable              |
| **Vessel Size Affected** | Small-to-medium arteries/arterioles | Small arteries/arterioles | Variable   | Large arteries            | Variable              |
| **Angiography**         | Negative                          | Negative                 | Positive   | Positive                 | Negative              |
| **MRI Findings**        | Cortical microhemorrhages         | Deep hemorrhages         | AVM nidus  | SAH, possible ICH        | Variable              |
| **Pathology**           | Amyloid deposition in vessel walls | Lipohyalinosis, microaneurysms | Tangled vessels | Saccular dilatation      | Inflammation          |
| **Age Group**           | Elderly                           | Elderly                  | Any        | Middle-aged/Elderly       | Variable              |
| **Prior Biopsy**        | Amyloid positive                 | Negative                 | N/A        | N/A                      | Negative              |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
CAA is a crucial cause of lobar hemorrhage in the elderly, often presenting with recurrent bleeds and associated with chronic cortical microhemorrhages on MRI. It is characterized pathologically by amyloid deposition in cerebral vessel walls. Differentiating CAA from hypertensive vasculopathy (which typically affects deep structures) and ruling out structural lesions like AVMs or aneurysms (via angiography) are key steps in diagnosis. (CAA 是老年人小叶出血的重要原因，通常表现为复发性出血，并伴有 MRI 上的慢性皮质微出血。其病理特征是淀粉样蛋白沉积在脑血管壁中。区分 CAA 与影响深部结构的充血性血管病，以及排除 AVM 或动脉瘤等结构性病变（通过血管造影）是诊断的关键步骤。)
